# The Real-Estate Lead-Response-to-Site-Visit Bible

**A practical bootstrap guide for Pune, India — from no customers to a repeatable managed service**

**Version:** 1.0  
**Research cut-off:** 3 September 2026  
**Founder assumed:** Age 29. Lives in Pune. Has a hybrid full-time job. Earns ₹1,00,000 per month in hand. Has ₹5,00,000 in savings. Can use up to ₹50,000/month.  
**Business model:** charge an implementation fee and a monthly managed-service retainer. The client owns and controls its advertising accounts, CRM, WhatsApp Business Account/number, DLT Principal Entity registration, headers/templates, telephony resources and platform usage. The vendor gets only limited role-based access for the agreed work.  

> **Read this first:** This guide tells you how to do the work. It does not guarantee results. Do not quit your job, borrow money, send messages to people in bulk, or spend all ₹5 lakh. Your first goal is two unrelated paying pilots. It is not a logo, company registration, chatbot, or SaaS product. The legal and tax sections are checklists for running the business. They do not replace advice from a Maharashtra lawyer, CA, or privacy professional. They also do not replace the client's own compliance review.

> **Launch-blocking legal gate:** You may run interviews, synthetic demos, and backend diagnostics if you follow employment and privacy rules. You must choose an operating model in [Section 30.2](#compliance). You must also have evidence for every gate that applies in Section 30.22. Until then, do not message or call real buyers, qualify them, or schedule site visits. If a Maharashtra lawyer does not clearly approve how you will run Model A, use Model B. Complete agent registration, competency certification, and project association before contacting buyers.

This easy-language edition keeps technical and legal terms because you will see them in tools, contracts, and laws. Each term is explained in simple words where it appears or in [the glossary](#glossary).

<a id="control-panel"></a>

## 0. Founder control panel — start here every week

### 0.1 The one-sentence strategy

> Set up and run a reliable enquiry-to-visit workflow that the client owns. Keep it backend-only unless you are registered and authorised for buyer-facing work. Make sure legitimate leads are captured, assigned, passed to people, scheduled, and measured. Do this without replacing the CRM or promising sales.

### 0.2 The order of operations

1. Check your employment agreement. Make sure it allows outside work.
2. Write down the exact backend and buyer-facing tasks, fees, and scripts. Get a written Maharashtra RERA opinion. Then choose Model A or B.
3. Interview 25 target customers before you build anything. Do not ask for live buyer data.
4. Sell a small paid diagnostic. Then sell a 21–30 day pilot that can start only after all launch gates are met.
5. Map the client's current process. Set a baseline, which is the starting measure.
6. Set up deterministic workflow automation first. This means clear rules produce the same result each time.
7. Add AI only when it is safer or more useful than fixed rules.
8. Run acceptance tests with synthetic, or made-up, data.
9. Get launch approval from named owners for business, CRM/IT, sales/site, and privacy/legal work.
10. Start live use with a small legitimate cohort, or group. Make sure people supervise it and define thresholds that will stop the launch.
11. Compare response, contact, qualification, and site-visit metrics. Do not claim that your work caused a result unless the evidence supports that claim.
12. Move the pilot to a retainer, or write down why it failed.
13. Repeat the work until five to ten customers show you what a standard product should be.
14. Only then should you hire in a meaningful way, advertise heavily, build software, or think about leaving your job.

### 0.3 This week's scoreboard

Copy this table into your weekly notes. Update it every Sunday.

| Measure | Weekly goal before the first client | Weekly goal after the first client | Actual result | Action to fix a gap |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| New suitable target accounts researched | 30 | 20 |  |  |
| Personal first messages or calls | 25 | 15 |  |  |
| Follow-up messages or calls | 25 | 20 |  |  |
| Requests for referrals | 5 | 3 |  |  |
| Discovery calls completed | 3 | 2 |  |  |
| Proposals for paid work sent | 1 | 1 |  |  |
| Pilot/retainer money collected | — | Track ₹ |  |  |
| Client leads processed without failure | — | Track count |  |  |
| Workflow failure rate | — | Target <1%; investigate every failure |  |  |
| Middle (median) time from lead arrival to assignment | — | Set for each client |  |  |
| Middle (median) time from lead arrival to the first attempt by a person | — | Set for each client |  |  |
| Site visits booked | — | Compare baseline with current result |  |  |
| Site visits confirmed/attended | — | Compare baseline with current result |  |  |
| Complaints, blocks and opt-outs | — | Track every one; a sudden rise means stop and investigate |  |  |
| Hours worked on the business | 10–15 | 12–18 |  |  |

### 0.4 Non-negotiable money rules

- Keep at least ₹3.5 lakh for personal emergencies. Use a different reserve only if your real essential expenses support it.
- Spend no more than ₹75,000 on the first 90-day test. Before you earn revenue, the absolute limit is ₹1 lakh.
- Before a customer pays to validate the idea, never buy lead lists, inventory, a franchise, expensive agency coaching, or annual software.
- Never pay client advertising or message costs from your own money. When possible, the client should pay each platform directly.
- Collect 50%–100% of the setup fee before you start configuration.
- Bill at clear milestones. Do not give the client an unsecured loan by doing unpaid work.
- A signed proposal is not revenue until the client pays. A verbal yes is not a sale.
- Keep your salary and business accounts, records, and devices separate.

### 0.5 Kill, continue and scale gates

| Gate, or decision point | Proof needed | Decision |
|---|---|---|
| Problem gate | At least 5 of 25 qualified interviewees separately describe the same costly process failure | If not, narrow or change the segment or problem |
| Payment gate | At least 2 unrelated customers pay; a discount is allowed, but a free pilot is not | If not, revise the promise, proof, buyer, or channel |
| Delivery gate | At least 95% of agreed test cases pass, and every failure alerts a person | If not, do not increase traffic |
| Outcome gate | At least one agreed leading metric improves, and consent or complaint results do not get worse | If not, check adoption, data, and process before you add AI |
| Retention gate | The client renews or gives a specific reason that makes economic sense for not renewing | If the client does not renew, record the root cause; do not hide it with new sales |
| Productisation gate | 5–10 customers need the same workflow, fields, and integration | Only then build reusable modules or micro-SaaS |
| Resignation gate | Owner cash after tax is ≥₹1.5 lakh/month for 9–12 months. No client is >30% of revenue. You have 12 months of personal runway, or money to live on | Keep the job until then |

### 0.7 How to use this as an operating system, not a textbook

Do not read at least 52,000 words once and expect to remember them. Use these four layers:

| Your situation | What to read/use now | What you should produce |
|---|---|---|
| Starting this week | Chapters 0, 30 and 38 | Legal limits, calendar, and actions for the first seven days |
| Getting ready for interviews/sales | Chapters 4–14 + Templates 41.2–41.10 | Account cards, interviews, diagnostic, proposal, and issue list for counsel |
| Building/delivering a pilot | Chapters 15–30 + Checklists 40.7–40.17 | Approved design, UAT, launch, operations, and incident evidence |
| Running the work each week | Sections 0.3, 27, 40.12–40.14 and Template 41.33 | One scoreboard, client actions, and founder decision log |
| Making scale/job/FIRE decisions | Chapters 31–39 + Checklist 40.20 | Evidence-based decision about cash, margin, renewal, and resignation |

Keep this book read-only as your standard. Copy each needed checklist or template into a dated client workspace. Fill it in, link the evidence, and record any planned difference in the decision log. Change the standard only after you review a lesson, a law or policy changes, or the same customer pattern appears more than once.

<a id="business-definition"></a>

# Part I — Business foundations

## 1. Business definition: what you sell and do not sell

### 1.1 The business in plain language

A property developer or brokerage pays for leads from portals, ads, referrals, calls, website forms, and walk-ins. These leads often arrive in different places. Some are duplicates. Some wait too long for an owner. Follow-up may not be consistent. The team may not be able to trace a lead to a site visit. You study this operating system and find the gaps. You connect approved sources to the client's CRM. You set up reliable follow-up and hand-off to people. Then you report what happened.

You deliver **a managed revenue-operations system**, which means a system that runs and tracks revenue work. You do not deliver only a bot.

### 1.2 The economic job you perform

Your system should help the client answer these five questions:

1. Did every legitimate enquiry reach one system of record, which is the main trusted system?
2. Did every lead go to a suitable person who was available?
3. Did each lead get a prompt, relevant, and allowed response?
4. Did the system record the person's intent and schedule the next action?
5. Can managers trace source → contact → qualification → booking → attendance → outcome?

### 1.3 What you can legitimately promise

Promise only work and metrics that are mostly under your control:

- connection of the agreed sources;
- creation or update of valid records;
- deterministic assignment logic, which uses fixed rules;
- start of the acknowledgement workflow when permission and channel rules allow it;
- clear paths for escalation and retry;
- visible lead status and activity;
- a workflow for reminders and confirmations;
- dashboards and a weekly review;
- written proof of better response time and more complete data;
- training and support within a defined SLA, or service level agreement.

### 1.4 What you must not promise

- Guaranteed bookings, sales, revenue, or return on ad spend.
- “Every lead contacted in five minutes” when you do not control the people needed to do it.
- A claim that AI knows availability, price, inventory, approvals, or legal facts unless it gets current approved data.
- Legal compliance certification unless a qualified professional accepts that duty.
- A promise that your system will replace salespeople.
- “Unlimited” changes, support, messages, or integrations.
- Better lead quality when you do not control media targeting or portal quality.
- Certain attribution, or proof of which source caused a result, when offline work or source tracking is incomplete.

### 1.5 Your position in the value chain

```text
Owner of lead source     Client's company               Your managed service
-----------------        -----------------              --------------------
Meta / Google /          Brand, projects, sales reps,   Review of the process
property portals /  -->  approvals, inventory, CRM  --> System connections
website / calls          and compliance                 Set up the workflow
                                                       Watch + report
                                                       Train + improve
```

The client stays responsible for its property claims, advertising accounts, sales conduct, licences, approvals, pricing, inventory, consents, and final messages. Your contract must clearly state who is responsible for each duty.

### 1.5A The legal operating model is part of the product

| Model | What the customer pays for | Who talks to the buyer and schedules visits | When you may use it |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A. Backend RevOps** | Audit, CRM/source integration, internal routing/tasks, dashboards, reliability, and staff training | Client's authorised staff | Use this by default while written RERA advice is pending; counsel must still review the exact scope |
| **B. Registered buyer-facing VisitOps** | Everything above, plus limited approved response, qualification, and visit coordination | Your authorised people or system under human control, with the right registration, certification, and association | Use only after you complete every Chapter 30 requirement |

Do not sell Model A and quietly deliver Model B. Labels such as “automation,” “consultant,” “retainer,” or “no commission” do not always make regulated work unregulated. What you actually do is what matters.

### 1.6 Business-model ladder

| Stage | What you sell | Reason for this stage | Do not move forward until |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0. Research | Sell nothing; run interviews only | Learn the words customers use and find costly failures | Five people confirm the same pain |
| 1. Diagnostic | Fixed-fee audit of the workflow/funnel | Start with low risk and create a baseline | The buyer agrees on the priority and data access |
| 2. Paid pilot | One project, one team, limited sources, and 21–30 days | Prove delivery and leading metrics | Acceptance tests and pilot report are complete |
| 3. Managed service | Monitoring, problem solving, reports, and approved improvements | Create repeat value and predictable revenue | SOPs, or standard operating procedures, support consistent delivery |
| 4. Productised service | Standard modules and packages | Improve margins and speed up onboarding | At least five clients have similar needs |
| 5. Software/IP | Reusable connector, dashboard, or vertical application | Grow beyond the hours you can personally work | Customers repeatedly pay to fix the same gap |

### 1.7 The smallest viable initial service

For the first client, limit the scope to:

- only one project or sales team;
- only one CRM;
- at most two lead sources;
- a single official message channel;
- a single set of assignment rules;
- a single qualification path;
- a single calendar/site-visit process;
- a single dashboard;
- support from a person during business hours;
- monitoring for four weeks.

Complexity grows roughly with `sources × projects × teams × channels × exception paths`. It does not grow only with lead volume. Set the scope and price with this in mind.

<a id="founder-constraints"></a>

## 2. Founder constraints, ethics and success criteria

### 2.1 Your advantage

- Your steady salary gives you time to reject bad clients.
- In Pune, you can meet developers, broker teams, and channel-partner networks in person.
- Your ₹5 lakh savings give you a safety cushion. Customer interviews need effort, not money.
- Hybrid work may give you time for meetings. Never use your employer's time or property.
- You can earn from a service before you build any software product.

### 2.2 Your constraints

- A full-time job limits when you can reply and visit client sites.
- This guide does not know how much technical, sales, or real-estate experience you have.
- You do not yet have case studies, references, or a reputation in this industry.
- Messaging, property advertising, and processing personal data create rules you must follow.
- One founder cannot provide 24×7 support while also selling, building, and training.

Build your offer around these facts. At first, sell implementation with monitored service during business hours. Do not sell a critical 24×7 call centre.

### 2.3 Ethical operating rules

1. Never make up claims about property scarcity, discounts, approvals, possession dates, investment returns, or testimonials.
2. Never scrape or buy consumer phone numbers for automated outreach.
3. When disclosure is appropriate, tell people that they are dealing with automation.
4. Always give people a clear way to reach a person for help.
5. Stop messages after a person opts out. Apply that suppression, or do-not-contact status, in every system.
6. Collect only the fields you need for the purpose you stated.
7. Do not guess protected or sensitive traits and use them for unfair targeting or routing.
8. Treat phone numbers, conversations, budgets, preferred locations, and property interests as personal data.
9. Let the client own and export its data. Never hold that data hostage.
10. Do not use an AI-generated answer about legal title, RERA status, price, inventory, or financial returns unless it uses approved source data and has human controls.

### 2.4 Personal success ladder

| Level | Proof | What it means |
|---|---|---|
| Learning | 25 qualified interviews | You understand the words customers use for the problem |
| Validation | Two unrelated paid pilots | There is some proof that customers will pay |
| Repeatability | Five successful implementations with similar scope | A focused service is starting to form |
| Side-income fit | ₹50k in monthly operating cash for six months | It is useful extra income, but cannot yet replace your job |
| Full-time option | ≥₹1.5L in monthly post-tax owner cash for 9–12 months | Think about switching only when you have enough runway and income from more than one source |
| Scale | Standard onboarding, written QA, and delivery that does not depend only on you | Hire people or turn the service into a standard product |
| Wealth engine | ₹2L–₹3L+ in monthly household/business surplus that you can invest | It could speed up the path toward ₹5Cr |

### 2.5 Founder red flags

Stop and review your plan if you find yourself:

- watching tutorials instead of talking to customers;
- building an AI agent for every channel before you have one paid pilot;
- lowering the price because the buyer does not understand the value;
- taking administrator access before a contract is signed;
- using your own WhatsApp number for the client's consumer messages;
- letting one client demand a custom platform while paying only a pilot price;
- counting client ad or message costs that pass through you as your revenue;
- reporting bookings without saying whether people attended or cancelled;
- hiding failures so the case study looks better;
- spending more because you worry that earlier spending will be wasted.

<a id="funnel"></a>

## 3. The complete lead-to-site-visit funnel

### 3.1 Canonical funnel

```text
Enquiry from a person who may be contacted
        ↓
Capture and record the original source
        ↓
Check data / filter spam / resolve duplicates
        ↓
Create or update CRM record + save audit event
        ↓
Assign an eligible owner + backup owner
        ↓
Send an allowed acknowledgement
        ↓
Person makes first attempt + records the result
        ↓
Check fit and match a project
        ↓
Offer a site-visit time
        ↓
Save the booking, project, date, owner, and attendees
        ↓
Confirm + remind + allow rescheduling
        ↓
Record attendance / no-show / cancellation
        ↓
Sales follow-up, future follow-up, or do-not-contact status
        ↓
Management dashboard and repeat improvement process
```

### 3.2 Stages and exit criteria

| Stage | What starts it | Action you must take | Rule for leaving the stage | Do not confuse it with |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| New | A valid enquiry arrives | Save it, add the time, remove duplicates, and assign it | The lead has an owner | Contacted |
| Acknowledged | An allowed system response is delivered | Name the brand and context, then offer a next step | Delivery status is saved | Contact by a person |
| Attempted | A rep calls or sends a message | Save the channel, time, and result | A disposition, or result type, is chosen | Connected |
| Connected | A person has a two-way conversation with the lead | Confirm identity and context, then learn what the person needs | A useful exchange takes place | Qualified |
| Qualified | The minimum agreed fit fields are saved | Match a project and agree on the next action | The lead meets the qualification standard | “Hot” only because a rep feels that way |
| Visit proposed | A suitable project and times are offered | Save the choices | The lead chooses or refuses | Booked |
| Visit booked | The exact time, site, and owner are agreed | Create the booking and confirmation | A unique booking ID exists | Attended |
| Confirmed | The lead confirms again | Prepare the host and travel details | The confirmation time is saved | Certain attendance |
| Attended | Check-in or the host confirms attendance | Save the attendees and result | Evidence is saved | Sale |
| No-show/cancelled | The booked visit does not happen | Save the reason and offer a new time if allowed | A next action is set, or the record is closed | Lost |
| Nurture | The lead is not ready, but permission and purpose allow later contact | Follow the approved schedule | The lead qualifies again, opts out, or reaches the time limit | Marketing forever |
| Closed-lost | A defined reason shows the lead is not suitable or was lost | Stop automation that no longer fits | The reason and date are saved | Delete the record |
| Opted-out/suppressed | The person withdraws permission, blocks you, or complains | Stop the related messages in every system | The suppression, or do-not-contact status, reaches every system | A simple “not now” |

### 3.3 Funnel equations

Use these letters:

- `L` = valid leads after duplicates are removed;
- `C` = leads that had meaningful contact;
- `Q` = leads that qualified;
- `B` = site visits that were booked;
- `A` = site visits that people attended;
- `S` = sales/bookings, if the client gives you reliable data.

Then use these formulas:

```text
Contact rate           = C / L
Qualification rate     = Q / C
Visit-booking rate     = B / Q
Visit show rate        = A / B
Lead-to-attended rate  = A / L
Sale rate              = S / A  or  S / L (label denominator clearly)
No-show rate           = no-shows / eligible booked visits
Speed-to-assignment    = assigned_at - received_at
Speed-to-first-attempt = first_human_attempt_at - received_at
```

Never mix cohorts, which are groups measured from the same starting point. A lead received on 31 August may attend in September. Report both counts for the event period and conversion for the lead cohort.

### 3.4 Bottleneck principle

Automation helps only when it fixes the current bottleneck, or main point of delay:

- Leads are missing → fix ingestion, or how leads enter the system.
- Assignment is slow → fix routing and staffing.
- Acknowledgement is fast but contact is low → improve the human attempt plan and data quality.
- Contact is high but qualification is low → check targeting, project fit, and the script.
- Qualification is high but booking is low → check availability, the offer, and rep behaviour.
- Booking is high but attendance is low → improve confirmations, travel details, clear expectations, and rescheduling.
- Attendance is high but sales are low → this is outside the first service scope; give the evidence to sales and project leaders.

Do not claim success only because response time improved when attended visits did not improve. Also do not claim success when bookings rose but no-shows rose sharply.

<a id="icp"></a>

## 4. Market map and ideal customer profile

### 4.1 Market segments

| Segment | Usual buyer | Size of pain | Possible budget | Time to sell | Difficulty of delivery | Starting priority |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| One local broker working alone | Owner | Medium | Low | Fast | Low | Avoid unless the broker gets an unusually high number of leads |
| Brokerage team, 5–20 callers/agents | Owner or sales head | High | Medium | 2–6 weeks | Medium | **Best segment to start with** |
| Channel-partner firm that serves projects | Founder or operations head | High | Medium | 2–8 weeks | Medium-high | Strong choice if it is clear which source gets credit for each result |
| Small developer, 1–3 active projects | Sales or marketing head | High | Medium-high | 4–10 weeks | Medium-high | Good choice after you have one case study |
| Mid-size developer with several projects | CXO or heads of CRM, marketing, and pre-sales | Very high | High | 2–6 months | High | Target later |
| Enterprise developer | Procurement, IT, legal, security, and sales | High | High | 4–12 months | Very high | Do not target this group first |
| Rental/resale aggregator | Founder or operations head | Medium-high | Medium | 2–8 weeks | High lead volume | Use a separate workflow; do not mix it with the new-launch offer at first |

### 4.2 Recommended beachhead ICP

Start with **Pune brokerage/channel-partner teams that have 5–30 sales users and at least 300 digital enquiries/month. They should have one decision-maker, an existing CRM or organised spreadsheet, and site visits they can measure**.

This is a good starting group because it has:

- enough leads to measure improvement;
- lost opportunities that cost enough to matter;
- a shorter buying process than large developers;
- people you can reach through local meetings and referrals;
- work simple enough for a side-business pilot;
- a buyer who can usually approve ₹25k–₹50k without a large-company buying process.

### 4.3 Qualification scorecard for target accounts

Give each area a score of 0–2. Contact accounts that score 14+ out of 20 first.

| Area | 0 | 1 | 2 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Monthly number of leads | <100 | 100–299 | ≥300 |
| Paid lead buying | None | Sometimes | Ongoing spending on portals/ads |
| Number of team members | 1–2 | 3–4 | 5–30 |
| System used now | No clear system or no access | Spreadsheet or basic CRM | CRM with gaps you can identify |
| Proof of pain | General curiosity | One story | Measured delays, missed leads, or no-shows |
| Access to a decision-maker | Unknown | Person who influences the choice | Owner or head of sales/marketing is available |
| Access to data | No export or administrator access | Some access | A practical export, API, or administrator path |
| Site-visit records | None | Manual or not consistent | Records exist but can improve |
| Approach to compliance | Wants spam or deception | Not sure | Will record consent and follow the rules |
| Ability to pay | Wants a free test | Can pay for a small pilot | Accepts the commercial pilot range |

### 4.4 Disqualifiers

Do not work with a client that:

- asks you to scrape consumer contacts or avoid DND or platform rules;
- cannot name the legal business or brand that sends the messages;
- expects false claims about a project, RERA, scarcity, returns, or price;
- has no person ready to call qualified leads;
- refuses CRM or data access but still demands guaranteed results;
- cannot define or record a site visit in a consistent way;
- gets fewer than roughly 100 leads each month and cannot combine enough leads for a useful test;
- demands 24×7 support but pays only a small pilot fee;
- wants a complex enterprise CRM replaced in four weeks;
- refuses an advance payment, contract, clear privacy duties, or change-control process;
- uses a project or advertising setup that its lawyer or compliance owner will not approve.

### 4.5 Buyer map

| Role | What matters to them | What worries them | Proof you should show |
|---|---|---|---|
| Founder/developer | Lost revenue, clear information, and cost | Paying for another tool that staff do not use | A simple funnel and money model |
| Sales head | Lead assignment, rep work, and visits | Unfair monitoring, poor leads, and impossible goals | Fair rules, a plan for use, and data about each result |
| Marketing head | Credit for each source and lead response | Sales blaming the quality of leads | Dashboard that follows each lead group from source to visit |
| Pre-sales/call-centre head | Work queue, staff, contact attempts, and scripts | Automation breaking the current workflow | A pilot with limited traffic and a way to roll back |
| CRM/IT admin | Reliable and secure work within system limits | Unapproved IT tools and fragile integrations | Architecture, test plan, least-privilege access, and logs |
| Legal/privacy | Consent, claims, data retention, and data processors | Complaints and processing without permission | DPA, or data processing addendum; data map; do-not-contact controls; and audit trail |
| Finance/procurement | Scope, invoice, and ROI | A retainer with no clear end or limit | Fixed pilot, acceptance criteria, and change control |

### 4.6 Market facts used carefully

MahaRERA's public search showed tens of thousands of registered agents. Established real-estate CRMs such as Sell.Do publicly claim 1,500+ customers and 25M+ processed leads. These facts show that the business category is large and already has strong competitors. They do not mean that every agent is a possible customer. Your service must work above or beside current systems. It must fix gaps in setup or staff use.

[Back to index](#table-of-contents)

---

<a id="validation"></a>

## 5. Customer research and demand validation

### 5.1 Research objective

Do not ask, “Would you buy AI automation?” Find a problem that happens often, costs money, needs a quick fix, and can be seen. Make sure someone with a budget owns that problem.

### 5.2 Interview sample

Complete at least these interviews:

- 10 owners of brokerages or channel-partner firms;
- 5 managers from sales or pre-sales;
- 5 callers or agents who work directly with leads;
- 3 people who run CRM or marketing operations;
- 2 property developers or senior project sales leaders.

Do not count friends who are only trying to be kind. Count them only if they fit the ICP and control or strongly influence a real process.

### 5.3 Interview request

> Hi [Name] — I am studying how Pune property sales teams handle online enquiries from the time they arrive until a site visit. I am not trying to sell software on this call. May I ask about your current process for 20 minutes? Afterward, I will share a one-page checklist for finding lost leads. I will not need any customer data.

### 5.4 Interview questions

Use these questions in order to collect facts:

1. Which projects and lead sources are running today?
2. About how many enquiries arrive in a normal week/month?
3. Use a made-up or redacted example to show me what happens from arrival to assignment.
4. Where does the team first find a new lead?
5. What does the team do outside office hours?
6. How does the team handle duplicate leads from different portals/campaigns?
7. How does the team choose an owner? What happens when that person is not available?
8. What response-time goal do you use? How do you measure it?
9. What do “contacted” and “qualified” mean here?
10. Which qualification fields does the team really use later?
11. How does the team book, change, cancel, and confirm a visit?
12. Who records attendance? Can managers trust that field?
13. At what point are leads most often lost?
14. Please describe the latest example.
15. What did that failure cost in ad spend, hours, visits, or management time?
16. What have you tried already? Why did it fail or why did staff stop using it?
17. Which CRM, phone, messaging, and calendar tools must stay in place?
18. Who is responsible for consent, message templates, and compliance approval?
19. Who can approve a four-week pilot to improve this process?
20. What proof would make that person renew the service?

### 5.5 Rules for interviewing

- Ask about the latest real example, not an imagined situation.
- Ask “what happened?” more often than you ask “would you?”
- Do not make a sales pitch during the first 80% of the talk.
- Record the talk only when the person clearly agrees. If the person does not agree, take notes that do not include personal data.
- Never ask for an export of consumer leads for research.
- Write down the exact words buyers use to explain their pain.
- Keep staff complaints separate from the priorities of the person who controls the budget.
- Note the workarounds people already use. Manual work that happens again and again is often stronger proof than a complaint.

### 5.6 Research repository

| Information to save | Example |
|---|---|
| Account / segment | ABC Realty / channel-partner team with 12 people |
| Person's role | Pre-sales manager |
| Leads each month | About 1,200 |
| Tools and process now | Portal emails → spreadsheet → CRM import |
| Problem that repeats | Leads from the weekend wait until Monday |
| Workaround used now | The manager sends the spreadsheet by hand |
| Result | The number of lost contacts is unknown; the owner raises complaints |
| How often it happens | Every week |
| Person who can buy | Founder |
| Metric wanted | Time until assignment and first attempt |
| What they tried before | They turned off the CRM workflow because it created duplicates |
| Compliance worry | The consent/source field cannot be trusted |
| Exact quote | Save a short paraphrase unless you have permission to record or quote |
| Next step | Audit meeting / not a fit / date for follow-up |

### 5.7 Evidence scoring

| Proof | Score |
|---|---:|
| The problem happened in the previous 30 days | +2 |
| It happens every week or more often | +2 |
| The buyer can give a number for the volume or cost | +2 |
| Staff already use a manual workaround | +2 |
| The person who controls the budget asks for a next step | +3 |
| The account pays for a related CRM, tool, or agency | +2 |
| The buyer agrees to give you baseline data with personal details removed | +2 |
| The buyer signs and pays for the pilot | +10 |
| A compliment, survey interest, or “sounds useful” | 0 |
| A request for a free custom build | -2 |
| A request to spam, scrape, or avoid consent | Reject the account |

### 5.8 Validation decision

After 25 interviews, make a table that shows how often each problem appears. Choose a problem that meets all these tests:

```text
happens often × costs enough to matter × urgent × measurable × data is available × buyer has authority × can be delivered in four weeks
```

If fewer than five qualified people separately describe the same pain, do not build anything. Interview a more focused segment or a different kind of buyer.

[Back to index](#table-of-contents)

---

<a id="offer"></a>

## 6. Positioning and offer design

### 6.1 Recommended positioning

**Category:** a service that sets up and manages real-estate lead operations / revenue operations, which is the work that moves and tracks leads.  
**Not:** a chatbot agency, bulk WhatsApp marketing agency, CRM replacement, lead seller, or digital-ad agency.

### 6.2 Positioning statement

> Pune property sales teams already pay for digital enquiries. But some teams cannot clearly track a lead from arrival to site visit. [Business Name] sets up and watches a response and booking workflow that uses the CRM as the main system. We do not add a general chatbot or replace the CRM. We work with the team's current tools. We record consent and hand-offs. We report the full path from source to attendance.

### 6.3 Core offer

> **Lead-to-Site-Visit Control System:** a setup with fixed limits and ongoing monitoring. It captures leads from agreed sources, resolves duplicates, and routes each lead. It supports allowed acknowledgement and qualification. It schedules, confirms, and reschedules site visits. It alerts people when needed and creates a weekly funnel report.

### 6.4 Offer components

| Part of the offer | What you deliver | Proof that the client can accept it |
|---|---|---|
| Baseline audit | Map of the current process, data dictionary, and starting report | The client signs off on the written assumptions |
| Source connection | Events from each agreed source enter staging/CRM | A test lead from every source shows the right source attribution, or source credit |
| Identity resolution | Rules to create, update, and review possible duplicates | Tests for duplicate records pass |
| Assignment | Rules for territory, project, language, and availability, plus a fallback | A test distribution follows the approved matrix |
| Response | Approved path for the acknowledgement and channel | The team tests the template, consent rule, and delivery status |
| Qualification | Shortest required order of questions and fields, with “unknown” allowed | Every branch and path to a person passes |
| Site visit | Choice of time, booking ID, owner, and status | Tests for booking, rescheduling, and cancellation pass |
| Reminders | Approved confirmation sequence with a time limit | Tests use the right templates and stop messages for suppressed contacts |
| Dashboard | Counts from source to each stage, conversion, timing, and exceptions | The dashboard matches the agreed sample records |
| Training | Sessions for administrators and reps, plus an SOP, or standard operating procedure | The team records attendance and handover |
| Monitoring | Alerts, problem log, and weekly review | The team completes an alert drill and receives the report |

### 6.5 Outcome hierarchy

Sell the highest result level that you can measure with good evidence:

1. **Reliability:** no valid event from an agreed source disappears without warning.
2. **Visibility:** every lead has a source, owner, status, and next action.
3. **Speed:** the team can measure and reduce delays in assignment and the first attempt.
4. **Consistency:** the team uses approved meanings for qualification and booking.
5. **Attendance:** a better confirmation and rescheduling process raises the show rate.
6. **Economics:** the client uses its own margins and conversion data to estimate recovered contribution, which is value after relevant direct costs.

Do not move straight to claims about revenue. Advertising, project-market fit, sales skill, price, inventory, and the client control too many later results.

### 6.6 Risk reversal without dangerous guarantees

You may offer:

- a paid diagnostic with fixed limits, with its fee credited toward implementation;
- a pilot with written acceptance criteria;
- payments at milestones linked to tested deliverables;
- more implementation time with no labour charge if an in-scope acceptance test fails, as long as client dependencies allow the work;
- the right to cancel the monthly managed service with 30 days' notice after the first term;
- a recorded export and handover when the service ends.

Do not offer:

- guaranteed sales or site visits;
- a refund based on later revenue that you cannot check or control;
- free support with no limit;
- guaranteed results without minimum lead volume, staffing, data access, and client compliance;
- only performance fees before attribution, or source credit, can be trusted.

### 6.7 Naming the offer

Choose a name that describes the result:

- Lead-to-Visit Control System;
- Site-Visit Pipeline Operations;
- Property Lead Response Desk;
- Enquiry-to-Visit Revenue Operations;
- Lead Leakage Audit + Recovery Workflow.

Do not use names that suggest the system can do legal or sales work on its own. Examples are “AI Closer,” “Guaranteed Booking Machine,” and “Human-Free Sales.”

[Back to index](#table-of-contents)

<a id="pricing"></a>

## 7. Packages, pricing and ROI

### 7.1 Pricing principle

Set the price based on risk, difficulty, and value in daily work. Do not base it on the number of automation boxes on a screen. A monthly “bot” price of ₹5,000 attracts clients with weak processes. It also leaves no money for discovery, QA, monitoring, support, or compliance.

### 7.2 Recommended offer ladder

The prices below are only starting ideas to test in Pune. They are not regulated tariffs or market averages for everyone. Show GST as a separate charge when it applies. Pass changing vendor costs to the client.

| Offer | Buyer it fits | Work included | Starting professional fee | Length |
|---|---|---|---:|---|
| Lead-loss diagnostic | Team does not know where leads fail | Interviews, funnel map, review of sample data, risk register, and roadmap in priority order | ₹15k–₹30k | 1–2 weeks |
| Paid test pilot | Brokerage/team with 5–20 users | One project, one CRM, ≤2 sources, one channel, main booking flow, and dashboard | ₹25k–₹50k setup + vendor use | 21–30 days |
| Main implementation | Small team with a confirmed need | Up to 3 sources, approved routing, messages, scheduling, reports, and training | ₹60k–₹1.25L setup | 4–8 weeks |
| Managed core service | Workflow that is stable | Monitoring, incident handling, monthly allowance for changes, and weekly dashboard/review | ₹20k–₹40k/month | At first, a minimum of 3 months |
| Managed growth service | Team with several sources | More workflows, tests, monthly improvements, and a stronger response SLA | ₹45k–₹75k/month | 3–6 months |
| Programme for several projects | Developer or large channel partner | Separate rules for routing and inventory, separate environments, oversight, and several teams | ₹1.5L–₹5L+ setup; ₹75k–₹2L+/month | Client's own buying process |

These starting prices assume a backend setup with strict limits. They also assume that client staff handle buyer messages unless the quote clearly includes the price and evidence for Model B. Buyer-facing work adds registration/certification, staff, supervision, control of content and channels, records, insurance, and the risk of incidents. Change the scope and price for that work. Do not hide these duties inside the same pilot fee.

### 7.3 Pilot scope boundaries

A proper pilot includes:

- a written baseline and target leading metrics, which are early measures of progress;
- acceptance tests that use synthetic, or made-up, data;
- a limited live launch;
- ongoing checks and a final report;
- one clearly named client owner;
- a written list of things the client must provide;
- clear responsibility for approving messages/templates;
- export and handover of data.

A pilot does **not** include integrations without a limit. It also does not include cleaning every old record or building a custom mobile app. It does not cover buying media, writing property ads or providing a call-centre team. It does not guarantee leads, include closing sales or provide support after the pilot period.

### 7.4 Bottom-up quote builder

Use a worksheet. Never make up a quote during the call.

```text
Professional fee = discovery and design
                 + standard implementation
                 + source connector work
                 + CRM configuration/API work
                 + messaging/template workflow
                 + scheduling/visit workflow
                 + data cleanup or migration
                 + dashboard/reporting
                 + training/change management
                 + QA and launch supervision
                 + project-risk contingency
```

| Cause of difficulty | Low | Medium | High |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lead sources | One built-in or form source | 2–3 APIs, webhooks, or email parsers with documents | Portals with no stable integration and many data formats |
| CRM | Clean setup with standard options | Custom fields or workflows | Old or custom CRM, poor data, or limited access |
| Projects | One | 2–3 | Many projects with changing inventory or rules |
| Routing | One queue | Rules for territory, project, or language | Work shifts, staff capacity, skills, channel-partner conflicts, and a complex fallback |
| Messaging | Current approved setup | New templates or setup | Several numbers or brands, weak consent proof, and high-volume campaigns |
| Scheduling | One site or calendar | Several hosts or sites | Limits on capacity, transport, events, and calendars that conflict |
| Reporting | Standard funnel | Reports by cohort, or comparable group, and source | Joining offline data, booking or sales data that cannot be trusted, and custom BI, or business intelligence |
| Governance | One approver | Sales + marketing + CRM | IT, legal, privacy, procurement, and outside agencies |

If two or more areas are “High,” do not make this your first pilot with a fixed price.

### 7.5 Founder cost floor

Work out your lowest internal hourly price, even if you do not show it to the client.

```text
Required monthly owner cash + business overhead + risk reserve
---------------------------------------------------------------- = internal hourly floor
realistically billable hours per month
```

Here is an example for when you still have a job:

```text
(₹50,000 desired owner cash + ₹15,000 overhead + ₹15,000 risk reserve)
÷ 60 genuinely deliverable hours = ₹1,333/hour internal floor
```

Suppose a pilot needs 45 hours. The tools directly cost ₹8,000 and you add 20% for unexpected work:

```text
(45 × ₹1,333 + ₹8,000) × 1.20 ≈ ₹81,600
```

This example shows why a very custom ₹25k pilot needs strict limits. You can also treat it as paid learning. Do not keep selling it at that price forever.

### 7.6 Payment structure

| Type of work | Suggested payment terms | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| Diagnostic | 100% before work starts | The deliverable is short and fixed |
| Small pilot | 60% before work starts, 40% before the live launch/final handover | Protects your cash while keeping payment linked to a milestone |
| Implementation | 50% at the start, 30% when configuration is approved, 20% at go-live/handover | Links payment to milestones you can control |
| Retainer | Paid monthly in advance; automatic reminder; right to stop service after a serious payment delay | Stops you from lending money to the client |
| Changing usage costs | Client-owned account/card or a deposit paid in advance | Costs that pass through you do not use your working capital |
| Large custom work | Paid discovery first; SOW with milestones | You cannot set a responsible fixed price for an unknown scope |

### 7.7 Pricing rules

- List your professional fees, taxes, and charges from other companies separately.
- Put clear volume assumptions in every quote. Include leads, users, sources, projects, messages, and support hours.
- Include a monthly limit for changes, such as two small setup changes or four hours. Do not promise “unlimited optimisation.”
- State what counts as urgent support.
- Charge a new onboarding fee if a client changes its CRM, brand/number, or architecture.
- Keep any yearly prepayment discount small. Offer it only after delivery is stable.
- Reduce the scope before you reduce the value of each unit.
- Never accept a fee based only on results for an unlimited time before attribution and client duties can be trusted.

### 7.8 ROI model

Use financial numbers that the client has checked. Clearly separate values that are **observed**, **estimated**, and **assumed**.

```text
Valid unique leads (L)
× incremental attended-visit rate (ΔA/L)
= incremental attended visits

Incremental attended visits
× verified visit-to-sale rate
× verified contribution per sale
= estimated incremental contribution

Net estimated value
= incremental contribution
 + labour/admin savings valued by client
 - incremental media/message/vendor cost
 - implementation and service fees
```

### 7.9 ROI sensitivity example — not a promise

Suppose the client confirms that it gets 1,000 valid leads each month. Its current lead-to-attended rate is 4.0%. After correcting for source and time period, the pilot cohort, or test group, reaches 5.0%. This means 10 more attended visits. The client also checks, on its own, that the visit-to-sale rate is 5% and the contribution per sale is ₹2 lakh:

```text
10 visits × 5% × ₹2,00,000 = ₹1,00,000 expected contribution/month
```

But if the visit-to-sale rate is only 2%, the estimate drops to ₹40,000. Show this as a sensitivity range, which shows how the result changes when an input changes. Do not say “₹1 lakh generated.” The client must approve the assumptions.

### 7.10 Metrics you can use for pilot commercial success

Choose two to four measures before launch:

- success rate for putting valid leads into the system;
- median and 90th-percentile time until assignment;
- median and 90th-percentile time until the first attempt by a person;
- share of leads that have a valid owner and next action;
- contact rate within a set time;
- qualified-lead rate based on a definition that does not change during the test;
- bookings for each qualified lead;
- rate of confirmed bookings;
- attendance, or show, rate;
- share of records with complete source attribution, or source credit;
- number of exceptions with no action and how old they are;
- complaint, block, and opt-out rate as a guardrail, or safety limit.

### 7.11 Gross-margin targets

| Stage | Useful general target | Reason |
|---|---:|---|
| First two pilots | May be only 10%–30% or may just break even | You are paying to learn; record every hour |
| Standard implementation | 40%–60% cash gross margin before founder salary/tax | Pays for QA, sales work, and fixes |
| Managed service | 50%–70% before founder salary/tax when delivery is stable | Repeat monitoring should become standard work |
| Custom enterprise work | Margin may be lower unless the client pays for discovery/change control | Coordination and risk take time |

Media, message, and software costs that pass through you are not high-margin revenue. Report them separately.

<a id="sales-pipeline"></a>

# Part II — Getting customers

## 8. Sales pipeline design

### 8.1 Your own CRM stages

Start with a simple CRM or spreadsheet. Give every stage a clear rule for leaving it.

| Stage | What puts it here | Rule for leaving the stage | Longest time before action |
|---|---|---|---:|
| Target | It appears to fit the ICP | Likely buyer is named, and there is a lawful business contact method | 7 days |
| Researched | Notes cover the account, project, clues about its tools, and your hypothesis | You send the first personal contact | 3 days |
| Contacted | You complete the first outreach | You get a reply or referral, reject the account, or set a follow-up date | A schedule of 15 business days |
| Discovery scheduled | A qualified person agrees to meet | The meeting takes place | 7 days |
| Discovery complete | You record the pain, process, authority, timing, and next step | Move to an audit/demo or reject the account | 2 days |
| Solution validated | The buyer confirms that the planned scope addresses the main need | The buyer asks for a commercial proposal | 3 days |
| Proposal | You give the buyer the written scope and prices | The buyer signs, clearly says no, or agrees to a dated next step | 14 days |
| Contracting | The buyer confirms the intent to buy | You receive the contract + advance payment | 21 days; allow longer only when you can see the buying process moving |
| Won | The payment has cleared | Hold the onboarding kickoff | 5 business days |
| Lost/nurture | You save a specific reason and a valid basis for future contact | Set an event/date to try again, or mark the contact as suppressed | Review each month |

### 8.2 Mandatory opportunity fields

- legal name and account name;
- website and office address;
- segment and number of team members;
- project(s) involved;
- name and role of the business buyer;
- business contact channel that was made public;
- source of your information about the prospect;
- your idea about the likely problem;
- estimated number of valid leads each month;
- CRM/tools used now;
- steps used to make the buying decision;
- expected dates;
- estimated package and value;
- next action with an exact date and owner;
- latest useful contact;
- reason the deal was lost or rejected;
- contact preference and opt-out status.

### 8.3 Pipeline mathematics

Start with the goal of one paid pilot each month. Work backward from it. Here is an example based on assumptions:

| Step | Conversion used for planning | Number needed |
|---|---:|---:|
| Paid pilot | — | 1 pilot |
| Proposal → payment | 25% | 4 proposals |
| Qualified discovery → proposal | 50% | 8 discovery calls |
| Positive talk → discovery | 50% | 16 positive talks |
| Targeted first contact → positive talk | 10% | 160 first contacts |

These numbers are planning assumptions. They are not benchmarks, or results that everyone should expect. Replace them with your real rate for the latest rolling 90-day period. If you contact only 20 accounts and do not get a client, the group is too small to tell you much.

### 8.4 Pipeline hygiene

- Every opportunity must have a next step and date.
- “Follow up sometime” does not count as a stage.
- Close deals that are no longer moving. A correct pipeline is more useful than a large one.
- Record why each deal was lost: no pain, no authority, no budget, bad timing, lack of trust, competitor, scope, compliance, or no reply.
- Review conversion for each segment and source. Do not look only at the total.
- Do not automate personal relationship steps until you can do them well by hand.

<a id="prospect-database"></a>

## 9. Building a lawful prospect database

### 9.1 Target-account sources

- MahaRERA's public directories of registered agents and projects;
- official websites of developers and brokerages;
- project ads and landing pages that are public;
- public CREDAI information and public member or event information from local industry groups, when their terms allow it;
- research and connections that you make by hand on LinkedIn; do not use banned scrapers or automation;
- Google Business profiles and office numbers that are public;
- introductions from media buyers, CRM implementers, photographers, property lawyers, CAs, and sales trainers;
- local property shows and business networking events;
- people from your interviews and earlier contacts who agreed to be contacted.

Information being public does not automatically give you permission to send automated consumer messages in bulk. Put **business accounts and professional buyers** in this database. Do not put in scraped phone numbers of people looking for property.

### 9.2 Research template

| Field | Information to save |
|---|---|
| Account | Legal name and trading name |
| Segment | Brokerage, CP, developer, resale, or rental |
| Area | Pune micro-market(s), or small local markets |
| Proof of an active project | Public URL and date |
| Likely lead sources | Ads, forms, or portals you can see; mark this as an inference, or informed guess |
| Sign of team size | Rough team size that is publicly visible; mark it as an estimate |
| Clues about current tools | Form provider, CRM references, or job posts; never work around access controls |
| Buyer | Name and role from a lawful public business source or referral |
| Contact method | Business email, office phone, or a social connection made by hand |
| Hypothesis | One possible lead-loss problem for this account |
| Trigger | New launch, hiring, campaign, CRM change, expansion, or event |
| Fit score | ICP score /20 |
| Source and date | Information needed to check the source and age of the record |

### 9.3 Trigger events

A useful trigger gives you a timely reason to make contact:

- launch of a new project or phase;
- a clear rise in ads that collect leads;
- hiring for pre-sales, call-centre, or CRM jobs;
- a new sales office or area;
- a move to a CRM;
- a public complaint about replies or customer messages;
- a property exhibition that will happen soon;
- one channel-partner team taking care of several projects;
- a new manager for sales or marketing operations.

Never pretend that you know someone. Never pretend to be a buyer so you can study the process.

### 9.4 Data minimisation

For prospecting, keep data about the business and only the professional details you need to make contact. Record every opt-out. Follow your retention policy and delete old personal contact details. Do not use personal phone numbers unless the person gave the number for business contact or you received it through a real referral with the right context.

[Back to index](#table-of-contents)

<a id="outreach"></a>

## 10. Outreach channels and scripts

### 10.1 Channel priority

| Order | Contact channel | How to use it |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | Warm referral | Has the most trust; ask related service providers and people you interviewed |
| 2 | Business email written by hand | Share one clear idea about the problem without pressure |
| 3 | Office/business phone | Find the person who owns the process and ask for a meeting |
| 4 | In-person event/meeting | Works well in Pune; bring a one-page diagnostic |
| 5 | LinkedIn contact made by hand | Research the person and make a thoughtful contact within the platform rules |
| 6 | Useful content or a case study | Builds trust and can bring people to you over time |
| Avoid | Cold bulk WhatsApp/SMS sent to scraped numbers | Creates risks from consent rules, spam, and harm to your reputation |

### 10.2 First-email formula

```text
True detail that matters to the account
+ possible problem in daily work
+ clear value with low risk
+ one small question or action
+ honest identity and respect for opt-outs
```

### 10.3 Cold email — owner/sales head

**Subject:** A question about [Project/Team]'s enquiry-to-visit process

> Hi [Name],  
> I noticed [specific, truthful trigger]. Teams that get leads from several portals/ads often struggle to match the source, owner, and site-visit status. This can happen even when the team already has a CRM.  
>  
> I help property sales teams map this path. I also set up a controlled lead-to-site-visit workflow around their current system. I am now speaking with Pune teams to learn where they lose the most leads.  
>  
> Do you own this process, or does someone in pre-sales/CRM operations own it? If this is relevant, I can share the 12-point checklist for lost leads and discuss it for 20 minutes.  
>  
> Regards,  
> [Real name] | [business/professional identity] | [phone] | [website/LinkedIn]

### 10.4 Trigger-based email

**Subject:** Question about routing leads for [New project]

> Hi [Name],  
> Congratulations on the [truthful launch/trigger]. A launch can quickly bring many leads. During that burst, it may be hard to see duplicate records, assignments after office hours, and the owner of each booking.  
>  
> I set up a limited four-week control layer in the team's current CRM. It covers agreed lead capture, backup assignment, site-visit status, and a dashboard for problems. It does not replace the CRM or guarantee sales.  
>  
> Would a 20-minute process audit with your pre-sales/CRM owner help? If there is no problem, I will say so.  
>  
> [Signature]

### 10.5 Follow-up sequence

| Business day | What to do | What to say or send |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | Personal email | Share an observation + ask a question |
| 3 | Office call | Find the process owner and mention the email |
| 6 | Helpful follow-up | Send the checklist or one clear question that can find a problem |
| 10 | Short note about a case or process | Explain one failure pattern and its control without making a bigger claim than the facts support |
| 15 | End the sequence | Ask if you should close the file or make contact again after a clear trigger |

Stop earlier if the person asks. Do not create ten follow-ups that become more forceful each time.

### 10.6 Follow-up email

> Hi [Name] — here is one useful test with any vendor. Take yesterday's enquiries. Check whether each one has (1) an original source/campaign, (2) a current owner, (3) a time for the first attempt by a person, and (4) a next action. Missing fields often show the first gap in control.  
>  
> If all of this is already correct, you probably do not need my service. If it is not, I can help review a sample with personal details removed. Would [two proposed times] work?

### 10.7 Break-up email

> Hi [Name] — I will close this thread so I do not bother you. If enquiry assignment, clear follow-up records, or site-visit attendance becomes important during a future launch, reply with “audit.” I will send the current checklist. Regards, [Name].

### 10.8 Office cold-call opener — Hinglish

> Namaste, main [Name] bol raha hoon. Main property sales teams ka lead se site visit tak ka process better banata hoon. Main CRM replace nahi karta. [Specific project/trigger] ke liye pre-sales ya CRM operations ka process kaun sambhalta hai? Main unse ek chhota process question poochna chahta hoon. Yeh call consumer lead ya property kharidne ke liye nahi hai.

When the call reaches the right person, say:

> Main ek baat samajhna chahta hoon. Jab multiple portal/ad leads aate hain, kya source, owner, first attempt aur visit status ek hi jagah sahi milte hain? Ya team inko haath se match karti hai? Agar data already clean hai, main aapka time nahi lunga. Agar gap hai, hum 20-minute process call mein checklist use kar sakte hain.

### 10.9 Referral ask

> You said that [specific problem] is common in teams that handle several projects. Do you know one sales/pre-sales operator who would give me an honest view of the process? I want one talk so I can learn. I am not asking to add that person to a campaign. If you are comfortable, please introduce us in one message so the person knows why I am writing.

### 10.10 Event conversation

> I fix process gaps between a paid enquiry and an attended site visit. I do not sell leads or CRMs. How does your team check that every new enquiry has an owner and a next action?

Then listen. Do not start listing features.

### 10.11 Outreach quality checklist

- [ ] The person could reasonably be a professional buyer. The person is not a consumer lead.
- [ ] The name, project, and observation are correct.
- [ ] Your identity and reason for contact are clear.
- [ ] You do not use a false case study, customer logo, or result.
- [ ] You do not send too many attachments or links.
- [ ] The CTA, or call to action, asks for one small decision.
- [ ] You respect an opt-out or negative reply.
- [ ] You record the activity and next step.
- [ ] You follow the platform terms and all commercial-message rules that apply.

[Back to index](#table-of-contents)

---

<a id="discovery-call"></a>

## 11. Discovery calls

### 11.1 Goal

The goal is to decide together whether there is a real problem and a good fit. The goal is not to show how much you know about technology.

### 11.2 Forty-five-minute agenda

| Time | What to discuss |
|---:|---|
| 0–5 min | Explain the context and roles, agree on the wanted result, and ask to take notes |
| 5–20 min | Review the current process through the latest real lead |
| 20–30 min | Discuss the effects, volume, baseline, and earlier attempts |
| 30–36 min | Learn how the decision will be made, limits on data/tools, the compliance owner, and timing |
| 36–41 min | Repeat the problem in simple words and test one focused solution idea |
| 41–45 min | Agree on a next step with a date, or clearly reject the account |

### 11.3 Opening

> Thank you. First, I will learn how the process works now and what you have tried. If I do not see a good pilot with fixed limits, I will tell you. If there is a fit, I will sum it up. Then we can decide whether an audit/demo with personal details removed is a useful next step. May I take notes about the process? I will not record the call unless we agree to that separately.

### 11.4 Process questions

- Show me how the latest enquiry moved from its source to its current status.
- Which timestamp shows when it first arrived?
- Can the same lead appear in more than one source/system?
- Who is responsible for finding and handling duplicates?
- What happens when the assigned rep is away or already has all the work they can handle?
- What does the team count as the first response: automated delivery, a call attempt, a connection, or a reply from a person?
- Which statuses are required? Which ones do managers trust?
- Who is allowed to change the project price, inventory, or content used in messages?
- How does the system create a booking ID?
- How do the security, host, and sales teams learn that a visit is planned?
- Who records attendance, and when do they record it?
- How does every system handle opt-outs, DND requests, and wrong numbers?

### 11.5 Business-impact questions

- About how many valid leads arrive each month after duplicates are removed?
- Using the same definitions each time, how many leads are contacted, qualified, booked, and attended?
- Which metric can you trust the least?
- What does a missed or badly delayed lead cost in daily work?
- How many staff-hours are spent on spreadsheets, forwarding, and matching records?
- Which management decision cannot be made today because the data is not complete?
- What must improve to make the pilot fee worth paying?

### 11.6 Buying questions

- Who is responsible for the result?
- Who manages the CRM, Meta/portal sources, messaging, and calendars?
- Who approves the wording, privacy work, and legal issues?
- Who signs the paid agreement and sends the advance payment?
- Must procurement or security review the work?
- Is there a deadline for a launch or event that is already planned?
- Could another project or internal plan stop this work?
- What is the next step if the audit proves that the gap exists?

### 11.7 Fit summary

> Let me make sure I understand. About [volume] enquiries come through [sources]. The main issue is [observed problem]. It causes [consequence]. You tried [attempt], but [reason]. The pilot must improve or measure [metrics]. It must work with [stack] and get approval from [roles] by [date]. Is any important part wrong or missing?

### 11.8 Qualification decision

| Area | What a pilot needs |
|---|---|
| Pain | Happened recently and repeats; it is not only general interest |
| Volume | Enough records to show how the process works |
| Buyer | A known owner who can influence the budget |
| Access | A practical path to redacted data, an API, or administrator access |
| Staffing | A team of people can act on the leads they receive |
| Compliance | The client agrees to check consent, message wording, and channel use |
| Measurement | A baseline exists, or the team can create one |
| Timing | A real period for doing the work; not interest with no end date |
| Payment | The buyer is willing to pay for a limited pilot |

### 11.9 End every call with one of four outcomes

1. Schedule the audit/demo with the needed stakeholders and data checklist.
2. Get a request for a proposal with a confirmed scope and decision date.
3. Set future contact with a real trigger/date and permission.
4. Reject the account and record the reason.

“I will send something and you can look” usually does not count as a real next step.

[Back to index](#table-of-contents)

---

<a id="audit-demo"></a>

## 12. Audit and demo

### 12.1 Never demo a fantasy

Use made-up names and numbers unless the client has signed a contract and approved a secure way to use data. Clearly show every assumption. Do not say that a portal/CRM integration exists until you check the technical access.

### 12.2 Fifteen-minute demo story

1. A made-up lead arrives from one agreed source.
2. The system checks the required fields and creates an ingestion event that cannot be changed.
3. The duplicate rules choose whether to create a record, update one, or ask a person to review it.
4. The routing rules choose a suitable owner and a backup.
5. An allowed acknowledgement names the brand and context, then offers a next action.
6. The lead gives the qualification details or chooses not to give them.
7. A person gets the context and takes over.
8. Someone books, reschedules, or cancels the site visit.
9. The system shows confirmation and attendance statuses.
10. The manager can see the funnel and the queue of exceptions, or records that need attention.

### 12.3 Demo narration

For each screen, explain these five parts:

```text
Problem → rule or safeguard → work a person must do → proof → what happens after a failure
```

Here is an example:

> The safeguard is not only “a WhatsApp was sent.” The system saves four facts separately: source event time, assignment time, delivery status and first attempt by a person. If assignment fails, the record goes into the exception queue and the manager gets an alert. The system does not quietly choose a rep without a rule.

### 12.4 Audit inputs

- a sample export with personal details removed and the agreed fields;
- agreed meanings for funnel stages and statuses;
- a list of lead sources and their owners;
- routing matrix, staff list, and working hours;
- approved IDs for the project and brand;
- current message templates and consent words;
- the current process for scheduling site visits;
- combined funnel data for 30–90 days, if it is available;
- the current way to handle exceptions and complaints;
- screenshots or configuration details when direct access is not yet suitable.

### 12.5 Audit output

| Problem found | Proof | Effect on the business | Severity | Recommended safeguard | Owner | Pilot/later |
|---|---|---|---:|---|---|---|
| Example: a new value replaces the source field | 17/50 records with personal details removed | Source attribution, or source credit, cannot be trusted | High | Save the first source + latest source in separate fields | CRM administrator | Pilot |

### 12.6 Demo close

> Which part would make a real difference to your team's work? Which part is not needed? What could stop a limited pilot for [project/source]? If we agree on the scope today, who else must approve data access, messages, and business terms?

[Back to index](#table-of-contents)

---

<a id="proposal"></a>

## 13. Proposal, negotiation and closing

### 13.1 Proposal structure

1. A short summary that uses the client's words.
2. Proof about the current process and a clear list of assumptions.
3. What the pilot will do and a clear list of what it will not do.
4. Exact limits for each source, project, team, channel, and workflow.
5. Items you will deliver and tests that will accept or reject them.
6. A measurement plan and guardrails, or safety limits.
7. Dates and the name of the person responsible for each task.
8. A short explanation of the architecture and data.
9. Assumptions about compliance and security.
10. Your fees, costs from other companies, taxes, and payment dates.
11. Rules for changes, support, and work that is not included.
12. Choices for moving to ongoing service, ending the work, and handing it over.
13. The date until which the offer is valid and the steps for signature/acceptance.

### 13.2 Strong executive summary template

> [Client] gets about [verified/estimated volume] enquiries from [sources]. The process now depends on [current method]. This creates [evidence-based gaps]. The planned [duration] pilot will cover [project/team]. It will connect [sources] to [CRM], set up [specific controls], and report [metrics]. It will not replace the CRM or create leads. It will not guarantee contact/site visits/sales. It will not change advertising/property facts that have not been approved. We will judge success with [acceptance and operating metrics].

### 13.3 Responsibility matrix

| Work | You | Client sales | Client CRM/IT | Client legal/privacy/marketing |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Map the process and set up the system | R | C | C | C |
| Credentials for sources/APIs | C | — | R/A | C |
| Legal basis for consent and privacy notice | C | — | C | R/A |
| Property facts, price, inventory, and claims | — | C | — | R/A |
| Routing matrix and list of available staff | C | R/A | C | — |
| Approval of message wording/templates | C | C | C | R/A |
| Response to leads by a person | — | R/A | — | — |
| Run the acceptance tests | R | C | C | C |
| Approve use in production, or with real leads | C | C | C | R/A |
| Monitor the system and report incidents | R | C | C | C |
| Handle consumer requests and complaints | C | R/A | C | R/A |

`R = responsible, A = accountable, C = consulted.` The client must stay accountable, or ultimately answerable, for its business messages and property claims.

### 13.4 Negotiation rules

- First ask which term is causing the problem: price, payment timing, scope, risk, or trust.
- Exchange one thing for another. Do not simply give in. For a lower price, offer a smaller scope, slower support, fewer sources, or a case-study promise with proper permission.
- Never remove duties that protect data or security.
- Never use a customer logo or case study as part of the deal unless the customer approves it.
- If the buyer asks for a free pilot, offer a smaller paid diagnostic instead.
- If the buyer asks for a guarantee, guarantee acceptance of tested deliverables. Do not guarantee sales.
- If procurement asks for a discount, ask for advance payment, a standard scope, and a longer term.
- Add every agreed change to the final SOW, or statement of work.

### 13.5 Common objections

| What the buyer says | Reply that helps find the real issue |
|---|---|
| “Our CRM already does this.” | “It might. The audit checks whether the agreed sources, owners, attempts by people, and visit results are set up and can be measured. If they are, you should not buy another layer.” |
| “We need more leads, not automation.” | “Then automation may not fix the main problem now. Before you spend more, should we measure how many current valid leads get an owner, a prompt attempt by a person, and a final result?” |
| “AI will talk incorrectly.” | “Facts with high risk can come only from an approved source or a person. We start with fixed routing rules and a limited set of questions. Every automated path has logs and a way to reach a person.” |
| “₹40k is expensive.” | “Which part does not give enough value? We can limit the pilot to one source and one project. But we should not remove QA, consent safeguards, or monitoring.” |
| “Do it free and we will pay after results.” | “I can give you the checklist at no cost. Setup and monitored testing are paid work. A smaller diagnostic costs ₹[amount]. If we continue, I can credit that amount toward the next work.” |
| “Guarantee ten site visits.” | “I do not control lead quality, rep action, or customer choice. I can guarantee the agreed setup work and acceptance tests. I can also report the leading metrics clearly.” |
| “Send a proposal.” | “I can send one after we confirm the project, sources, CRM, lead volume, stakeholders, success measure, and decision process. Without those facts, it would only be a general estimate.” |

### 13.6 Closing checklist

- [ ] The owner of daily operations confirms the problem and scope.
- [ ] An early check shows that the technical access path can work.
- [ ] The buyer, approvers, and legal entity that will pay are named.
- [ ] The acceptance criteria are in writing.
- [ ] The client's required work and dates are in writing.
- [ ] Your fees are separate from vendor use and tax.
- [ ] Both sides agree on the steps for the contract/SOW/DPA.
- [ ] Both sides agree on the advance invoice and kickoff date.
- [ ] Work with real users or data does not start until the contract, payment, and access requirements are met.

<a id="contracts"></a>

## 14. Contract and scope controls

### 14.1 Document stack

| Document | What it is for |
|---|---|
| Proposal/order form | Short business summary and signatures |
| Master Services Agreement (MSA) | Main legal terms that apply across projects |
| Statement of Work (SOW) | Exact project, scope, deliverables, dates, fees, and acceptance rules |
| Data Processing Addendum (DPA) | Data roles, instructions, security, subprocessors, deletion, and incidents; a subprocessor is another company that processes data for the service |
| Access authorisation | Approval for named systems, accounts, and roles |
| Register of approved messages/content | Templates, facts, and owners that the client has approved |
| Change request | Effect on cost, time, and risk when work is outside the SOW |
| Handover/termination record | Exports, credentials, deletion or return of data, and problems still open |

Ask a lawyer qualified in Maharashtra to adapt your first reusable set. Do not copy legal clauses from the internet without review.

### 14.2 SOW essentials

- legal entities and contact people;
- start date, end date, and pilot term;
- exact project, brand, team, and area;
- exact source systems and the way each one will connect;
- exact CRM instance and modules;
- message channels and the numbers/accounts that the client owns;
- assumptions about the number of leads, users, and messages;
- workflows and a clear list of work that is not included;
- deliverables and cases that will accept or reject them;
- system environments and the way the launch will happen;
- items the client must provide and deadlines for its replies;
- fees, tax, changing costs, and payment milestones;
- support hours, response goals, and meanings of severity levels;
- the method for controlling changes;
- renewal, ending the service, and handover;
- rights to use a case study or logo only after separate, clear, written permission.

### 14.3 Contract protections to discuss with counsel

- mutual confidentiality, which means both sides protect private information, and rules for allowed disclosure;
- ownership of IP, or intellectual property, that each side had before the work;
- the client's ownership or licence for the setup and data, plus your right to reuse general know-how without any client data;
- a warranty limited to professional work and the written acceptance criteria;
- a clear statement that you do not guarantee leads, visits, sales, ad results, or legal advice;
- the client's promise that it has the needed rights and permissions, gives lawful instructions, and provides correct content;
- data-processing roles and written instructions;
- approved subprocessors, or other companies that process data, and issues about storage or transfer across borders;
- security duties that match the level of risk;
- duties to report an incident and work together on it;
- return or deletion of records after the contract ends, except for records the law requires you to keep;
- allocation of indemnity, which decides who covers certain losses, after a lawyer reviews it;
- a limit on liability and suitable exclusions based on fees and risk; never assume that a clause from a template can be enforced;
- rules for late payment, stopping service, and collection;
- force majeure, which covers certain events beyond a party's control, and outages on third-party platforms;
- a non-solicit or non-circumvention term only when it is reasonable and a lawyer approves it;
- the law that applies, place for legal action, and method for settling disputes;
- duties for confidentiality, payment, IP, and data that continue after the agreement ends.

### 14.4 Change-control rule

The client must make a change request when it changes any item below:

- the source/portal or API method;
- the CRM, vendor, or instance;
- the WhatsApp number, business, or brand;
- the project or area;
- the routing or qualification model in an important way;
- the amount of data to move;
- the meaning of a dashboard measure;
- the launch volume or support hours;
- a security or compliance requirement;
- the target date after the client delays something it must provide.

Use this change-request template:

```text
Requested change:
Reason/business outcome:
Current SOW reference:
Impact on design/security/compliance:
Additional fee:
Timeline impact:
Testing required:
Approvers:
Effective date:
```

### 14.5 Client-delay rule

State that the schedule will move when the client delays credentials, approvals, templates, data, staff, or testing. Define a dormant period, which is a set time with no progress. After that period, pause the project. Charge for restarting or planning it again when that is suitable. Never leave a project “almost live” with no end date.
