<a id="onboarding"></a>

# Part III — Delivering the service

## 15. Client onboarding

### 15.1 Definition of “onboarded”

Receiving the advance does not mean the client is onboarded. Onboarding is complete only after you document the responsibilities, scope, systems, data, approvals, environments, measures and authority to launch.

### 15.2 Onboarding stages

| Stage | Output | Owner |
|---|---|---|
| Commercial handoff | Match the final proposal, SOW, payment and promises | Founder |
| Governance setup | Name the sponsor, project owner, admins, legal/privacy approver and sales champions | Joint |
| Access planning | List the systems, roles, credential method and client approvals | Technical leads |
| Baseline/data | Receive the definitions and the minimum redacted or aggregate data | Client data/CRM owner |
| Process workshop | Approve the current-state and future-state maps | Joint |
| Build plan | List the backlog, milestones, tests, dependencies and risks | You |
| Approval register | Approve content, project facts, templates, data purpose and retention | Client accountable owners |
| Launch readiness | Sign off on acceptance, training, monitoring and rollback | Joint |

### 15.3 Kickoff agenda — 60 minutes

1. State the business problem, pilot boundary and non-goals again.
2. Introduce the decision-maker, operations owner, CRM/IT admin, sales champion and content/privacy approver.
3. Confirm the projects, locations, teams, work hours and lead sources.
4. Confirm the system of record. Also confirm who owns each account.
5. Review how access will work. Never ask anyone to paste a password into chat or email.
6. Set final definitions for valid lead, duplicate, attempt, contact, qualified, booked, confirmed, attended and opt-out.
7. Approve the baseline period and pilot cohort.
8. Review how the client will approve messages, consent and property information.
9. Confirm the dates for the build, user acceptance testing, launch and review.
10. Review known risks, dependencies, the escalation path and actions for the next 48 hours.

### 15.4 Stakeholder register

| Role | Name | Decision rights | Routine channel | Emergency channel | Backup |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Executive sponsor |  | Decide scope, budget and escalations |  |  |  |
| Operational owner |  | Decide process and daily priorities |  |  |  |
| CRM/IT admin |  | Decide configuration and access |  |  |  |
| Sales champion |  | Decide routing, scripts and adoption |  |  |  |
| Marketing/source owner |  | Manage campaign and source metadata |  |  |  |
| Legal/privacy approver |  | Decide consent, notices, content and data use |  |  |  |
| Finance/procurement |  | Manage the PO and payment |  |  |  |
| Your delivery owner |  | Manage the build and support |  |  |  |

### 15.5 Access register

| System | Client owner | Environment | Access role | Purpose | Granted | Expiry/review | Revoked |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| CRM |  | Sandbox/prod | Least privilege | Set up and test the workflow |  |  |  |
| Lead source |  | Test/prod | Integration/API | Receive the agreed events |  |  |  |
| Messaging |  | Test/prod | Template/integration | Send approved messages |  |  |  |
| Calendar/scheduler |  | Test/prod | Service account | Manage slots and bookings |  |  |  |
| Reporting store |  | Test/prod | Scoped read/write | Measure funnel results |  |  |  |

Rules:

- Use named accounts created by the client when possible. OAuth or service accounts are also good options.
- Never let several people share one admin login.
- Turn on multi-factor authentication when the system supports it.
- Keep secrets in an encrypted secrets manager or platform credential store. Do not keep them in spreadsheets, prompts, source code or screenshots.
- Write down why you need each permission.
- Remove permissions that are no longer needed after setup.
- Revoke access when the contract ends. Get confirmation that this was done.

### 15.6 Client dependency tracker

| Dependency | Owner | Needed by | Status | Impact if late | Escalation date |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---:|
| Signed data-processing terms |  |  |  | Cannot access personal data |  |
| CRM sandbox/API credentials |  |  |  | Cannot build |  |
| Source test lead facility |  |  |  | Cannot accept ingestion |  |
| Approved routing matrix |  |  |  | Cannot assign leads |  |
| Approved message templates |  |  |  | Cannot send messages |  |
| Sales roster and shifts |  |  |  | Cannot test the fallback |  |
| Site calendars/capacity |  |  |  | Cannot book visits |  |
| Privacy notice/consent decision |  |  |  | Cannot send live messages |  |
| UAT participants |  |  |  | Launch will be late |  |

### 15.7 Handover from sales to delivery

Before delivery starts, compare these four documents one line at a time:

- discovery notes;
- proposal;
- signed SOW;
- invoice/payment milestone.

Create a “promise register.” If the sales wording promises more than the SOW, explain the difference in writing at once. Hidden expectations lead to extra work that does not make a profit.

<a id="process-audit"></a>

## 16. Current-state process audit

### 16.1 Audit principle

Do not automate a process only as the manager describes it. Watch what really happens across the source, the system and frontline staff.

### 16.2 Evidence hierarchy

1. A system event or log with a timestamp.
2. CRM activity or history.
3. A redacted source record or message delivery event.
4. A screen-share demo by the person who does the work.
5. A written SOP.
6. A manager's memory.
7. Assumption.

Mark the evidence class in your audit.

### 16.3 Source inventory

| Source | Event path | Format | Original timestamp | Unique IDs | Consent/context evidence | Failure notification | Owner |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Meta lead form | Native connector/API/webhook/manual export |  |  |  | Form, campaign and version |  |  |
| Google lead form | Connector/API/email/export |  |  |  | Form, campaign and version |  |  |
| Property portal | Vendor-specific API/email/CRM connector/export |  |  |  | Context for the portal enquiry |  |  |
| Website | HTTPS form/webhook |  |  |  | Notice, checkbox and form version |  |  |
| Inbound phone | Telephony/manual CRM |  |  |  | Call context and recording notice, if it applies |  |  |
| Walk-in/referral | Authorised manual entry |  |  |  | Source and permission record |  |  |

Never say an API is available until the client or vendor confirms its documentation, credentials, limits and allowed use.

### 16.4 Current-state swimlane

Map every step with this structure:

| Step | Lead/customer | Lead source | Automation/integration | CRM | Pre-sales rep | Manager | Failure/exception |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Enquiry submitted | Gives details | Creates an event |  |  |  |  | What happens if the webhook or export fails? |
| Lead assigned |  |  |  | Sets the owner | Gets the task | Watches the queue | What happens if no owner is eligible? |
| First attempt | Answers or does not answer |  |  | Records the activity | Calls or sends messages |  | What happens if the number is wrong or no one answers? |

### 16.5 Status audit

Ask these questions about every status:

- Who sets it?
- Which event that you can observe allows it?
- Is it reversible?
- Which time does the system store?
- Is a reason required?
- Does it trigger automation?
- Does it stop another automation?
- What happens if a user skips it?
- Does management trust it?
- Did it mean something different in the past?

Replace unclear labels such as `Hot`, `Interested`, `Follow-up`, `Visit Done`. Use written rules or supporting fields instead.

### 16.6 Baseline data-quality tests

Run these tests on a small dataset that the client approved:

| Test | Formula | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Required-field completeness | non-null valid values / eligible records | Shows whether the workflow can work |
| Source completeness | records with original source/campaign / valid leads | Shows whether attribution is reliable |
| Owner completeness | active owner / valid leads | Shows whether the queue is healthy |
| Next-action completeness | open leads with dated next action / open leads | Shows whether follow-up is disciplined |
| Duplicate candidate rate | likely duplicate clusters / records | Shows an inflated funnel or work conflicts |
| Invalid-phone rate | invalid format/unreachable classification / leads | Signals data or lead quality |
| Status-age distribution | age since last valid transition | Shows leads stuck in the pipeline |
| Activity lag | first human attempt minus received time | Sets the response baseline |
| Booking completeness | bookings with project, time, host and status / bookings | Shows whether the attendance workflow is ready |
| Suppression conflict | opted-out records with later outbound activity | Shows a compliance risk |

### 16.7 Timing analysis

Report the median and the 75th and 90th percentiles. Do not report only averages. Split the results by:

- source/campaign;
- office-hours vs after-hours;
- weekday/weekend;
- project;
- owner/team;
- new vs duplicate;
- lead quality class;
- cohort week.

A fast median can hide a group of very slow cases. The 90th percentile often shows leads that nobody worked.

### 16.8 Baseline freeze

Write down:

- date range;
- time zone (`Asia/Kolkata` and the preference to store times in UTC);
- eligible sources/projects;
- exclusions and missing records;
- exact numerator/denominator definitions;
- the data extraction query or export version;
- known quality limitations;
- the person who approved the baseline.

Never use new definitions to recalculate an old baseline without clearly saying so.

### 16.9 Future-state design workshop

Follow this order:

1. Remove unnecessary steps.
2. Make ownership and definitions clear.
3. Fix data capture.
4. Add deterministic automation. This means automation that follows fixed rules.
5. Add monitoring. Also add a human process for exceptions.
6. Add limited AI only when it improves a step that you tested.

If you automate a broken process, it fails faster and the failure is harder to see.

<a id="architecture"></a>

## 17. Reference technical architecture

### 17.1 Architecture principles

1. **CRM-first:** keep the client's approved CRM as the main system of record for operations.
2. **Event traceability:** keep the source event ID, time received, processing result and correlation ID. These fields let you trace an event.
3. **Idempotency:** a retry must not create another copy of a lead, message or booking.
4. **Deterministic before generative:** use clear, fixed rules for routing, consent, eligibility, status and suppression before using generative tools.
5. **Human accountability:** automation can help. Named people still own sales decisions and exceptions.
6. **Least privilege:** access only the systems and fields that the SOW requires.
7. **Fail visible:** send failures to a queue that people monitor. Never drop a failure without notice.
8. **Reversible rollout:** include feature flags or cohort controls and a rollback plan in the launch.
9. **Client ownership:** the client should own its brand identities, messaging numbers, source accounts and main CRM.
10. **Minimise copies:** do not export a whole CRM dataset into a handy spreadsheet unless needed.

### 17.2 Logical architecture

```text
 APPROVED LEAD SOURCES
 Meta | Google | portals | website | inbound call/manual
                  │
                  ▼
        INGESTION / ADAPTER LAYER
  authentication • signature check • schema validation
  source event ID • received timestamp • raw-event quarantine
                  │
                  ▼
       NORMALISATION + POLICY GATE
  phone/email normalise • required fields • consent/context
  spam/test filter • project/source mapping • suppression check
                  │
                  ▼
       IDENTITY / IDEMPOTENCY LAYER
 exact duplicate rules • fuzzy review queue • create-or-update
                  │
                  ▼
        CLIENT CRM — SYSTEM OF RECORD
 lead/contact • original source • owner • status • next action
                  │
        ┌─────────┼──────────┐
        ▼         ▼          ▼
 ROUTING/TASKS  MESSAGING  SITE-VISIT SERVICE
 rules/fallback approved    availability/booking/status
        │         │          │
        └─────────┼──────────┘
                  ▼
        EVENT / METRICS / EXCEPTION LOG
 delivery • attempts • transitions • errors • opt-outs
                  │
         ┌────────┴─────────┐
         ▼                  ▼
   OPERATIONS QUEUE     MANAGEMENT DASHBOARD
   human resolution     cohorts/SLA/funnel/quality
```

### 17.3 Component ownership

| Component | Preferred owner | Your role |
|---|---|---|
| Advertising/portal account | Client | Connect only the approved events |
| Domain and website form | Client | Set up a documented, secure endpoint or form change |
| CRM and production data | Client | Administer and connect only the agreed scope |
| WhatsApp Business account/number | Client | Set it up only with written permission |
| SMS header/templates/telemarketer relationship | Client principal entity | Give technical help, but do not act as the client's identity |
| Scheduler/calendar | Client | Set up rules for resources and owners |
| Automation workspace | Mature clients should own it when possible | Build and manage it through named access; export it when leaving |
| Custom code repository | Depends on the contract; the client should ideally have access | Keep versions and handover rights |
| Monitoring/issue tracker | Both sides can see it | Run it and report from it |

### 17.4 Event envelope

Every integration event should include at least these fields:

```json
{
  "tenant_id": "from-authenticated-endpoint-configuration",
  "provider_event_id": "provider-stable-id-or-null",
  "idempotency_key": "documented-stable-derived-key",
  "payload_hash": "sha256-of-canonical-relevant-payload",
  "event_type": "lead.created",
  "event_version": "1.0",
  "source_system": "example_source",
  "source_account_id": "client-owned-account",
  "occurred_at": "2026-09-03T12:30:00Z",
  "received_at": "2026-09-03T12:30:04Z",
  "correlation_id": "end-to-end-trace-id",
  "payload": {},
  "consent_context": {}
}
```

The event envelope is immutable, which means it must not change. Get `tenant_id` from the authenticated endpoint or account mapping. Do not get it from customer text. Use a stable provider ID when possible. If the provider has no stable ID, create a source-specific fallback from source fields that never change. Use fixed rules to create it. Store the exact rule and version. Never make a random ID for each delivery, because a retry would then look like a new event. Keep changing state, attempt counts and errors in separate processing-attempt records. A payload hash can find a “same ID, changed body” conflict. It does not replace authentication.

Do not send the raw object to an LLM or normal logs when it contains personal data you do not need. Redact or tokenise the data when possible.

### 17.5 Idempotency strategy

Use a different key for each kind of job:

- ingestion idempotency: `tenant_id + source_system + source_account + stable_provider_or_derived_event_key`;
- CRM identity candidate: `tenant_id + normalised phone/email + client-approved context`;
- outbound action: a unique, saved `tenant_id + send_intent_id`, created before calling the provider;
- booking: a unique, saved `tenant_id + visit_id`; use a separate unique claim for the slot or resource capacity;
- provider webhook/status event: `tenant_id + provider_event_or_status_id`.

Processing pattern:

```text
receive event
→ authenticate and validate
→ insert or claim the tenant-scoped unique idempotency key in one atomic step
→ allow only the winning transaction to schedule side effects
→ make a conflicting worker get the existing state and safely acknowledge or defer
→ run steps that are atomic or can be undone
→ add attempt and result events
→ retry temporary failures with backoff
→ send failures that used all retries to the human exception queue
```

A separate `SELECT/check → send → mark complete` sequence is not safe. Two workers can pass the check at the same time. Enforce a database unique constraint, transactional outbox or claim, compare-and-set, or a similar atomic primitive. Save a send intent before calling a message provider. Then attach the provider message ID and add every later status event. This lets you investigate an unclear timeout without sending the message again without checking.

### 17.6 Retry classification

| Failure | Retry? | Action |
|---|---|---|
| Read/idempotent request timeout, HTTP 429 or temporary 5xx | Yes, within the limit | Use exponential backoff with jitter; follow `Retry-After` |
| Unclear timeout on a non-idempotent create/send | Do not retry without checking | Check and match by the saved intent or deterministic ID, or use documented provider idempotency; otherwise ask a person to review |
| Authentication expired | Limited | Alert the admin; pause the affected workflow; do not keep calling the API |
| Invalid payload/missing mapping | Do not repeat automatically | Quarantine it and ask a person to correct it |
| Permission denied | No, until access is fixed | Alert the client admin |
| Duplicate/idempotency conflict | Do not create a new side effect | Get the existing result and match the records |
| Template rejected/policy block | No | Stop this path; alert the content or platform owner |
| Invalid/opted-out recipient | No | Set the right suppression or invalid state |
| Downstream outage | Yes, within the set window | Queue the work safely; notify people when it crosses the SLA limit |

Save the client and work fields in the queue or DLQ: `tenant_id`, work/event ID, workflow/version and state. Save the attempt count, first and last attempt, `next_attempt_at`, and maximum attempts and age. Also save the terminal reason, last safe error code, correlation ID, and lease or claim owner and expiry. Add a circuit breaker for a dependency that keeps failing. Also set a controlled recovery rate and a provider-cost limit. Before replaying a DLQ, check the current permission and state again. Get a dry-run count and approval from a named person. After replay, match the records.

### 17.7 Environments

| Environment | Data | Purpose | Outbound messaging |
|---|---|---|---|
| Development | Synthetic data only | Build the logic | Off or mocked |
| Test/UAT | Synthetic data or a very small approved test dataset | Let the client accept the work | Only test recipients or an approved sandbox |
| Production canary | A small, approved live cohort | Watch real use | Limited and watched |
| Production | Only the approved scope | Run the normal service | Keep policy and consent gates on |

Never test a new template, prompt or routing rule on the whole live database.

### 17.8 Non-functional requirements for a pilot

Agree on realistic values. Do not copy these examples without checking them:

| Requirement | Example pilot target |
|---|---|
| Ingestion availability | Monitor all the time during the agreed service window; list vendor dependencies |
| Processing latency | Record and assign 95% of valid events from supported sources within the agreed seconds or minutes |
| Data loss | Allow no silent data loss; put failed events in the exception queue |
| Duplicate side effects | Allow no duplicate outbound messages or bookings in the acceptance set |
| Recovery | Write the steps for replaying and matching records |
| Auditability | Use a correlation ID to link the source, CRM, message or task, and booking event |
| Alerting | Send a critical failure alert to a named person; set a target time to acknowledge it |
| Support | Clearly limit the founder's first service hours and response targets for each severity |
| Security | Use MFA, least privilege, safe secret storage, encrypted transport and access reviews |
| Retention | Keep raw payloads and logs for the shortest needed time, and document that time |

### 17.9 Buy/configure/build decision

| Situation | Default |
|---|---|
| CRM already supports rule | Set up the rule inside the CRM |
| Reliable supported connector exists | Use it after you review permissions, limits and security |
| Simple orchestration across documented APIs | Use a managed automation platform |
| Sensitive/high-volume/complex logic | Use a reviewed custom service or enterprise tool |
| Portal has no authorised integration path | Use an approved email, export or manual process; do not scrape or bypass controls |
| Client wants new CRM because adoption is poor | Find the process and adoption problems before replacing it |
| Generative AI proposed for simple condition | Use fixed rules instead |

<a id="data-model"></a>

## 18. Canonical data model

### 18.1 Modelling rule

Keep **person**, **enquiry**, **project interest**, **activity** and **site visit** as separate records. One person may send several enquiries. The person may also like several projects and book several visits. If you put all of this in one flat spreadsheet row, you lose history and attribution.

### 18.2 Core entities

| Entity | Purpose | Key relationship |
|---|---|---|
| Person/contact | Stores identity and communication choices | Can link to many enquiries, interests and activities |
| Lead/enquiry | Stores one inbound request and its context at one time | Links to a source, campaign and possible person |
| Event ledger | Stores a source receipt that is authenticated and cannot change, plus its idempotency claim | Uses one row for each provider or derived event key within a tenant |
| Processing attempt | Stores a changing execution lease, attempt, next retry and result | Many attempts can link to one event or work item |
| Project | Stores an approved property or project reference | Links to inventory versions, content versions and visit sites |
| Project interest | Stores a person's needs and fit for one or more projects | Links a contact to a project |
| Assignment | Stores who owns the work and why | Links a lead to a rep or team and includes timestamps |
| Activity/attempt | Stores a call, message, task or human interaction | Links the lead or contact, the actor and the result |
| Consent/preference | Stores notice, permission, purpose, channel and proof of withdrawal | Decides whether communication is allowed |
| Site-visit booking | Stores the full life cycle of the slot, site, host and status | Links the interest, lead or contact, and sales owner |
| Capacity claim | Reserves a slot or resource in one transaction | One unique active claim stops two bookings from using the same capacity |
| Calendar mapping | Stores the versioned link between a visit and a provider event | Allows fixed-rule creation, updates and reconciliation |
| Outbound send intent | Saves the planned recipient, purpose and rendered message before the provider call | A unique send ID links the workflow, eligibility decision and provider result |
| Message status event | Stores a provider status notice that cannot change | Ordered and late events produce one current status |
| Reconciliation run/item | Stores the watermark, counts, mismatches and repair decision | Shows whether independent systems agree |
| Exception | Stores a failed policy, integration or data step | Links the event, severity, owner and solution |

Every operations record has a `tenant_id` that cannot change. Get it from authenticated configuration. Include it in every unique constraint, foreign key, cache key, queue key, object-store path, query and authorisation check. Never trust a tenant identifier found in customer text or an unverified payload. Never treat an object ID that anyone can guess as proof of authorisation.

### 18.3 Lead/enquiry fields

| Field | Type | Required? | Notes |
|---|---|---:|---|
| `tenant_id` | Immutable reference | Yes | Sets the authenticated client boundary; include it in every key and query |
| `lead_id` | Immutable identifier | Yes | Identifies the lead inside the CRM or system |
| `source_event_id` | Text | Yes when provided | Supports idempotency and audit |
| `source_system` | Controlled value | Yes | Meta, Google, portal, web, call, referral, manual |
| `original_source` | Controlled value | Yes | Never replace this value |
| `latest_source` | Controlled value | Optional | Keep this separate from the original source |
| `campaign_id/adset_id/ad_id/form_id` | Text | If available/allowed | Keep the exact IDs because names can change |
| `utm_source/medium/campaign/content/term` | Text | If present | Check how the system handles length and characters |
| `occurred_at` | UTC timestamp | Yes | Records when the source created it |
| `received_at` | UTC timestamp | Yes | Records when your endpoint got it |
| `crm_created_at` | UTC timestamp | Yes | Records when the CRM saved it |
| `person_id/contact_id` | Reference | After resolution | Do not automatically merge identities when you are not sure |
| `project_interest_raw` | Text/code | Optional | Keeps the choice exactly as submitted |
| `mapped_project_id` | Reference | If safely mapped | Record the mapping version |
| `assigned_owner_id` | Reference | After routing | Identifies the current owner |
| `assignment_reason` | Controlled value | Yes | Territory/project/language/round-robin/fallback/manual |
| `status` | Controlled value | Yes | Use the fixed status dictionary |
| `status_reason` | Controlled value | Required for terminal states | Do not use only an unstructured reason |
| `next_action_at` | Timestamp | For open lead | Keeps the queue organised |
| `processing_state` | Controlled value | Yes | received/validated/quarantined/persisted/failed |
| `correlation_id` | Text | Yes | Lets you trace the work from start to finish |

### 18.4 Person/contact fields

| Field | Rule |
|---|---|
| Name | Store the value the person submitted; do not invent a longer name or gender |
| Phone | Keep the raw value secure; also store a normalised E.164-style value when it is valid |
| Email | Normalise it carefully; keep the raw value if an audit needs it |
| Preferred language | Ask the person or use a clear form value; do not guess from a name or religion |
| Preferred channel/time | Store the person's clear choice when provided |
| City/current location | Collect it only when you need it for the stated purpose |
| Budget range | Allow optional or unknown; use ranges the client approved |
| Purchase timeline | Use set ranges and allow unknown |
| Purpose | Use self-use, investment, other or unknown only when the client truly needs this field |
| Communication state | eligible, channel-limited, opted-out, blocked, invalid, disputed |
| Suppression reason/date/source | Require these details when communication is suppressed |

### 18.5 Consent/preference record

| Field | Description |
|---|---|
| `subject/contact_id` | Identifies the person involved |
| `channel` | WhatsApp, SMS, phone, email, etc. |
| `purpose` | Names the exact service, marketing or visit coordination purpose |
| `brand/controller` | Names the client identity shown to the person |
| `status` | granted, withdrawn, expired, disputed, unknown |
| `captured_at` | Records the time |
| `source` | Names the source, such as a form, inbound message, recorded interaction or portal context |
| `notice_version` | Keeps the exact version of the privacy or consent wording |
| `evidence_reference` | Gives an access-controlled link or ID for lawful evidence |
| `scope` | Lists the projects or categories and the type of communication |
| `withdrawn_at` | Records the time, if it applies |
| `suppression_propagated_at` | Records when suppression finished spreading to every system |

Never change `unknown` to `granted`. The client or legal owner must decide which action is allowed for every source and purpose.

### 18.6 Activity/attempt fields

- the activity ID and provider ID;
- the lead or contact ID;
- actor type: human/system;
- actor ID/role;
- channel;
- purpose;
- the start, sent, delivered, answered and completed timestamps that apply;
- direction: inbound/outbound;
- the attempt number in the defined sequence;
- the result, using approved values;
- the next action and its owner;
- a recording or transcript reference only when it is lawful and approved;
- the template and version for an automated message;
- events for failures, blocks, complaints and opt-outs;
- the correlation ID.

### 18.6A Outbound-send and delivery-status fields

Create `OutboundSendIntent` before you call a provider. At minimum, include `tenant_id` and the unchangeable `send_intent_id`. Add the lead/contact, channel, named business/project and purpose. Add the eligibility-decision ID/time, template/rendering/version/language and recipient token/reference. Also add the scheduled time, state, attempt/cost cap, correlation ID and payload hash. Store sensitive rendered content only when needed. Protect it when you store it.

Provider callbacks create `MessageStatusEvent` rows that cannot change. Each row must include the tenant, send intent, provider message/status-event ID, status, provider timestamp, received timestamp, raw-evidence reference and signature/auth result. Use a written state machine, priority order and provider time to calculate the visible status. A late duplicate `sent` event must never replace a `delivered` or `read` event that already has evidence. Keep failures, reasons and corrections. Do not delete the history.

For a planned later reminder or resend, create a new send intent. Link it to the earlier one and record the business reason. Do not avoid suppression or frequency limits by changing any idempotency key.

### 18.7 Site-visit fields

| Field | Required rule |
|---|---|
| `visit_id` | Immutable unique ID |
| `lead/contact/interest_id` | Links the visit to its context |
| `project_id` and `site_id` | Use approved references, not only free text |
| `start_at/end_at/timezone` | Stores the exact slot |
| `host_owner_id` | Names the responsible person or team |
| `party_size` | Use it only when operations need it; avoid extra details |
| `transport_requested` | Uses an optional controlled value |
| `booking_source` | Human, web, WhatsApp flow, call, reschedule |
| `booked_at` | Records the time |
| `status` | proposed/hold/booking_pending/booked/confirmed/booking_exception/rescheduled/cancelled/expired/declined/released/no-show/attended/completed |
| `capacity_claim_id/calendar_mapping_id` | Required for automated booking; gives a lasting tenant-scoped link with an opaque provider mapping |
| `confirmation_at/by` | Stores proof of confirmation |
| `attendance_at/by/evidence` | State who may mark a visit as attended |
| `cancellation/no-show reason` | Uses a controlled value and an optional note |
| `previous_visit_id` | Links the chain of rescheduled visits instead of deleting history |
| `next_action_at/owner` | Required after a result that is not final |

### 18.8 Source attribution model

Keep these ideas separate:

- original acquisition source;
- latest re-engagement source;
- enquiry-specific source and campaign;
- a referral or source reported by the person, if collected;
- offline influence if known;
- the attribution model and version used in a report.

Do not replace the original source when an old lead sends a new form. Do not promise perfect attribution across devices, offline calls and channels you do not manage.

### 18.9 Duplicate-resolution hierarchy

1. An exact replay of the source event ID → treat it as the same event and create no new lead side effect.
2. The exact normalised phone or email appears with the same form or project within a short time → treat it as a likely duplicate enquiry. Keep the event. Update or review it as policy says.
3. The same phone with a different project, time or source → treat it as a possible match to the same person, but possibly a new enquiry or interest.
4. Only a similar name → never merge it automatically.
5. A family or office shares the phone → require a person to decide; do not assume an identity.
6. Opt-out states conflict → use the safest suppression that applies until someone reviews it.

Keep a merge log. Record the records involved, the rule or user, the timestamp, the fields kept and the way to reverse the merge.

### 18.10 Data classification

| Class | Examples | Control |
|---|---|---|
| Public business | Project URL, public office number | Protect accuracy and track the source and date |
| Internal operational | Routing rules, team roster, funnel metrics | Limit access by role |
| Personal | Name, phone, email, preferences, conversation | Limit the purpose and access; use a retention and deletion process |
| Higher-risk | Financial documents, identity documents, recorded calls, detailed family/finance data | Avoid this data unless essential; require stronger approval, encryption and access logs |
| Secrets | API tokens, passwords, signing secrets | Use a dedicated encrypted secret store; never put them in CRM notes or prompts |

### 18.11 Status dictionary template

| Status | Objective definition | Who may set | Required evidence/fields | Allowed next states | Automation triggered | Automation suppressed |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| New | A valid, unique enquiry from a supported source has been saved | System/admin | source, timestamps, contact route | assigned/quarantined/invalid | assignment | nurture |
| Qualified | The approved minimum fit details were collected, and a person or process confirms the standard | Rep/system under rules | qualification fields/version | visit-proposed/nurture/lost | scheduling prompt | generic qualification |
| Attended | An authorised host or check-in confirms an in-person or approved online visit | Host/manager | visit ID, timestamp | follow-up/completed | sales follow-up task | pre-visit reminders |

Define every status for this client before you build the system.

<a id="workflow-library"></a>

## 19. Workflow library

### 19.1 How to read and implement a workflow

Use the same contract for every production workflow. Do not accept a diagram that shows only the successful path.

| Contract item | Mandatory answer |
|---|---|
| Workflow ID and version | Give it a stable ID such as `WF-01 v1.3`; name the owner and effective date |
| Business purpose | State the exact operations result; never write only “automation” |
| Trigger | Name the exact event, schedule or human action that starts the workflow |
| Preconditions | List consent and channel eligibility, required fields, project state and work hours |
| Input schema | List fields, types, maximum lengths, allowed values and the source of truth |
| Idempotency key | Explain how to spot a replay without sending or creating something twice |
| State transition | Name the allowed current state, next state and evidence to write |
| Decision rules | List fixed rules in order and give the version |
| Side effects | List each CRM record, task, message, calendar booking, alert or file the workflow creates |
| Timeouts/retries | State which failures retry, the delay or backoff, the attempt limit and the dead-letter action |
| Human owner | Name the role that receives an unclear case or failure; set a response target |
| Audit event | Record the correlation ID, timestamp, rule or template version and result |
| Suppression | List opt-out, complaint, do-not-call, invalid contact and all other stop conditions |
| Success metric | Give one measurable numerator and denominator or a latency definition |
| Acceptance tests | Test success, rejection, duplicate, late, out-of-order and failure cases |
| Rollback | Give the feature flag or off switch, queue steps and last known safe process |

### 19.2 Standard processing envelope

Follow this order for every inbound event. A tool may use different names, but keep the same controls.

```text
receive → prove source → record time → save raw reference → check → standardise
       → check consent and suppression → resolve identity and event → map project and source
       → create or update CRM → assign → acknowledge or create a task
       → write audit event → watch → compare systems

Any step that cannot recover → exception queue → named person → correct or replay → proof of closure
```

Never send a message until the event is safely saved and controls prevent duplicate side effects.

### 19.3 Workflow catalogue

| ID | Workflow | Trigger | Primary output | Human exception owner |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| WF-01 | Capture and prove the source of an event | Webhook, import or manual submission from an approved source | Saved event with a correlation ID | Integration admin |
| WF-02 | Check and quarantine | New saved event | Valid standard payload or a reason for quarantine | CRM/data admin |
| WF-03 | Resolve duplicates and people | Valid payload | Decision about an existing or new person and enquiry | CRM steward |
| WF-04 | Map the project and source | Standard enquiry | Approved project and source attribution that cannot change | Marketing ops |
| WF-05 | Check whether communication is allowed | Proposed action for a contact and channel | Decision to allow, limit or suppress | Client privacy/marketing owner |
| WF-06 | Assign an owner | Eligible new or open lead | Named owner, reason and SLA clock | Sales manager |
| WF-07 | Acknowledge an inbound lead | Valid, allowed inbound context | Approved acknowledgement, rep task or both | Assigned rep |
| WF-08 | Escalate an SLA breach | Assignment or attempt timer ends | Escalation, reassignment or manager task | Sales manager |
| WF-09 | Record qualification | Lead answers or rep updates the record | Structured fields and the next action | Assigned rep |
| WF-10 | Hand off a hot lead | A high-intent or urgent condition | Alert a person with context; pause the bot | Assigned rep/manager |
| WF-11 | Propose or book a visit | Qualified lead who is interested | Reserved slot and booking record | Booking coordinator |
| WF-12 | Confirm and remind | Booking reaches its planned time offsets | Confirmation state and reminders that are allowed | Booking owner |
| WF-13 | Reschedule or cancel | Customer or host asks for a change | Kept history and a new or final state | Booking owner |
| WF-14 | Record attendance and result | Visit time passes or host checks the person in | Approved result and follow-up task | Site host |
| WF-15 | Nurture or re-engage | Approved group and purpose | A sequence with an end time, or suppression | Marketing owner |
| WF-16 | Handle an opt-out or complaint | Stop request, complaint or block event | Suppression sent to every system at once | Privacy/marketing owner |
| WF-17 | Handle a delivery or API failure | Failed dependency or side effect | Retry or exception record; never lose it without notice | Integration admin |
| WF-18 | Change roster, inventory or project data | Approved master-data change | Safe update to routing or content | Client admin |
| WF-19 | Compare systems each day | Scheduled job | List of differences among source, CRM and messages | Service operator |
| WF-20 | Handle a deletion, export or access request | Verified and authorised request | Controlled completion and audit record | Client privacy owner |

### 19.4 WF-01 — capture and authenticate a lead event

**Purpose:** accept events only from approved sources. Keep enough proof to compare systems. Acknowledge events quickly while keeping control.

| Element | Specification |
|---|---|
| Trigger | A webhook request, authorised API poll, watched client mailbox or export, or authenticated manual form |
| Required metadata | The source, event ID when available, event time, receipt time, signature or authentication result, schema version and correlation ID |
| Authentication | Check the provider signature or token and the expected endpoint; reject failed authentication without creating side effects |
| Durability | Save the event or reference before later actions; redact or avoid extra personal data in logs |
| Immediate response | Return the success or failure response the provider requires before its timeout; run slower work later when the provider supports it |
| Idempotency | Use the provider event ID when possible; otherwise create a documented stable key from source identifiers, never only a name |
| Invalid input | Quarantine it with a reason that a machine can read; alert only when the severity or volume reaches the set limit |
| Metric | Count accepted, rejected, duplicate and quarantined events; measure receipt delay and the source comparison gap |

Acceptance cases include a valid signed event and an invalid signature. Test missing phone and email, an unexpected field, a Unicode name and a very long field. Also test a provider retry, two copies at the same time, a late event, an out-of-order update and a temporary CRM outage.

For a Meta webhook path, follow the current official contract exactly. Do not guess that a general bearer token is enough. Handle the GET subscription challenge. Keep the raw request body. Check the `X-Hub-Signature-256` HMAC-SHA256 before parsing the data or making side effects. Map the WABA, phone, form or account ID to an authenticated tenant configuration. Process every entry, change and message in a payload that contains several items. Enforce limits for content type, bytes, schema and depth. Claim each provider event in one atomic step before you acknowledge that it was safely received. These are examples for this provider. Before you build, check the fields, algorithms and versions again in the current Meta documentation.

### 19.5 WF-02 — validate, normalise and quarantine

Validation asks, “Can this payload enter the standard pipeline?” It does not decide whether the person is a good sales prospect.

1. Check that the required structure is present and the source is allowed.
2. Keep the raw data or source reference in controlled storage. Never change the only copy.
3. Remove harmless extra spaces. Put timestamps and the time zone into the standard form.
4. Normalise the phone carefully. Do not invent a country code when it is unclear.
5. Check the email format. Do not claim that the mailbox exists.
6. Map the project and campaign with versioned lookup tables.
7. Escape or encode untrusted text for the place where it will go. Never run content from the payload.
8. Set a missing optional field to `unknown`. Do not guess a value.
9. If a missing business mapping can be fixed, quarantine the item instead of throwing it away.
10. Save the processing result and reason with the correlation ID.

| Quarantine reason | Automated action | Human action |
|---|---|---|
| Unknown project/form | Hold outbound messages; alert the mapping owner | Map it to an approved project or mark it invalid |
| No usable contact route | Create a record only when client policy requires it | Review source quality; do not keep guessing |
| Invalid phone but valid email | Allow action only through an eligible channel | Correct it only with authorised evidence |
| Schema changed | Pause the affected source path | Check provider documents and release a tested mapping |
| Unsupported attachment/document | Do not open or forward it automatically | Use an approved secure process only if it is truly needed |
| Suspicious content/injection | Treat it as data, not an instruction; quarantine it if risky | Ask security to review it |

### 19.6 WF-03 — identity and duplicate resolution

Remove repeat events before you match people. These are two different problems.

```text
same event ID?                 → replay; do not create or send again
same strong contact identifier?→ may be the same person
same person, new enquiry?      → keep the new enquiry and attribution
uncertain match?               → keep separate records and review; never auto-merge in a way that loses data
```

Side-effect rule: before assigning or creating a message, insert or claim a unique tenant-scoped event record in one atomic step. You may use an equal database transaction instead. A check followed by a separate action is not a lock. If two events arrive at the same time, only the transaction winner may create the first acknowledgement. Keep both deliveries available for audit.

### 19.7 WF-04 — project and source mapping

Keep a versioned mapping table with `source account → form/campaign → project → site → active dates → approved owner`. If there is no active mapping with one clear meaning:

- keep the original IDs and raw project text that the person submitted;
- send the item to a neutral review queue;
- do not invent project, price or availability;
- do not send a project-specific message;
- close the exception only after a correction that the client approved.

### 19.8 WF-05 — communication eligibility

Before every outbound action, check the channel and purpose at that time. Do not check only when the lead is first created.

| Check order | Question | If “no/unknown” |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | Are the client or brand identity and intended recipient both clear? | Hold it and review |
| 2 | Does permission or another client-approved basis support this source, purpose and channel? | Do not send it automatically |
| 3 | Is there an active opt-out, complaint, do-not-call, block or legal hold? | Suppress it |
| 4 | Does the platform and policy allow this message type, context and template now? | Use an allowed option or ask a person to review |
| 5 | Is the contact data valid and still inside the approved retention period? | Correct, limit or delete it as policy says |
| 6 | Is the frequency below the limit the client approved? | Delay or suppress it |
| 7 | Is this content or template version approved, and is the project data current? | Hold it and ask for approval |

Write a clear decision code such as `ELIGIBLE-INBOUND-SESSION`, `SUPPRESSED-OPTOUT`, or `HOLD-CONSENT-UNKNOWN`. Do not record only `false`.

### 19.9 WF-06 — assign the lead

You must be able to explain each routing decision. Check required eligibility before you balance the workload:

1. Project/site eligibility.
2. The person's role and current roster status.
3. Territory or language, but only when operations require it and the data was clearly collected.
4. Work hours and availability.
5. Who owns an existing active relationship.
6. Capacity limit and fair or round-robin distribution.
7. The fallback queue and its manager.

Write the owner, assignment time, rule version and reason in one atomic step. Never leave a lead in `New` just because every rep is unavailable. Send it to a fallback queue that someone monitors.

Make the claim through a transaction-safe queue or cursor. You may instead use a conditional update that checks the expected unassigned state and version. If both the CRM and the integration can assign leads, choose one as the authority. Otherwise, set clear priority and guards that stop loops. A stale worker that loses the compare-and-set must read the winning value. It must not replace that value. For a manual reassignment, record the actor, reason and previous owner.

### 19.10 WF-07 — acknowledge and create the first-action task

An acknowledgement does not prove that a person made contact. Store these times separately:

- `system_acknowledged_at`;
- `first_attempt_at` and the channel;
- `first_connected_at`;
- `first_meaningful_response_at`;
- `owner_accepted_at`, if used.

The message should name the client brand or project. It should say why the person got it. It should set a realistic expectation and offer an easy way to reach a person. It must not make unsupported claims. At the same time, create a task for the owner. Include context and a due time.

### 19.11 WF-08 — SLA timer and escalation

Define every clock clearly.

| Clock | Start | Stop | Pause/exclusion |
|---|---|---|---|
| Assignment latency | A valid enquiry is saved | An owner is assigned | The item is quarantined because it is invalid or has a mapping problem |
| First-attempt latency | Owner assignment or valid receipt; choose one | A real outbound attempt is recorded | Closed hours that the client approved, if you report both raw time and business-hours time |
| First-contact latency | A valid event is received | The customer and a person connect in both directions | Never stop the clock only because a message was delivered |
| Qualification latency | A valid event is received | A qualification result under the fixed definition | You may report a later time requested by the customer on its own |

Escalation example: create the owner's task at T+0. Send a reminder at the limit the client approved. Then alert the manager. Reassign automatically only if policy allows it and no active conversation exists. After that, use the exception queue. Do not keep messaging the lead just because the rep missed an internal SLA.

### 19.12 WF-09/WF-10 — qualify and hand off

- Ask one easy question at a time.
- Allow `unknown`, “not decided,” free text and help from a person.
- Never call a person “unqualified” only because they did not answer a sensitive or unneeded question.
- Sum up the answers before booking.
- Stop automation when you see high intent, distress, a complaint, negotiation, a legal or finance question, or an interpretation with low confidence. Assign the case to a person.
- Include these fields in the handoff: lead identity, source/project, exact latest message, structured facts, unclear points, consent/channel state, promised callback/next action and conversation link.
- Let the person see why the alert fired. Let them record how they resolved it.

### 19.13 WF-11/WF-12/WF-13/WF-14 — visit lifecycle

[Chapter 22](#site-visits) gives the full state machine for a site visit. One rule is critical. Confirm with the customer only after you have safely reserved a slot in the calendar or capacity source of truth. A time you only proposed is not a booking.

### 19.14 WF-15 — nurture and re-engagement

Nurture is optional. It has more risk than answering an active enquiry. Require:

- a client-approved group, purpose, permission or other basis, and set of templates;
- exact start and end dates, plus a maximum frequency;
- current inventory and project facts;
- exclusion of an open sales conversation, recent contact, booked visit, sold or lost result, complaint and opt-out;
- monitoring of results and negative signals;
- an automatic stop after the agreed sequence; and
- a person who renews the campaign, instead of an endless loop.

### 19.15 WF-16 — opt-out, block and complaint

People can ask you to stop in normal language. Do not require one special keyword. When you receive a believable stop request:

1. Acknowledge it only when that is suitable and allowed.
2. Record the channel, purpose or scope, source text or event, time and status.
3. Cancel automated messages and nurture jobs that are waiting.
4. Send the suppression to the CRM, messaging and campaign systems.
5. Keep the minimum proof of suppression. This stops someone from importing and contacting the person again.
6. For a complaint or a system-wide spike, alert the responsible client owner.
7. Require a written, authorised override. Never let a salesperson remove the suppression without a record.

In acceptance tests, use words with the same meaning in English, Hindi and the Marathi forms the client approved. Also test punctuation, lower and upper case, voice-to-text errors and a path to a person.

### 19.16 WF-17 — failure, retry and replay

| Failure type | Default response | Do not do |
|---|---|---|
| Timeout/temporary `5xx` | Use limited exponential backoff with jitter, provider guidance and idempotency | Retry forever or too fast |
| Rate limit | Follow the retry details, slow the flow and alert people if the backlog lasts | Start many requests at once |
| Validation/`4xx` | Do not retry without checking; quarantine it with a reason | Keep paying for or sending the same invalid request |
| Auth/permission | Stop the affected connector; page the admin or security owner | Put the secret in a log or rotate it without coordination |
| CRM written, message uncertain | Ask the provider for the status or match it with a stable ID | Send a second message “just in case” |
| Message delivered, CRM write failed | Keep the provider ID; repair the CRM audit state | Lose the proof or send again |
| Dependency outage | Queue work within the approved retention and capacity; tell users or the client if needed | Pretend the SLA was met |

For every replay, record the original event ID, operator, reason, changes, new attempt and result. For a bulk replay, first get a dry-run count. If it could contact customers, also require a second approver.

### 19.17 WF-18 — master-data changes

Treat the project, price, inventory, address, sales roster, work hours and message templates as controlled content. For changes with important effects, require one person to make the change and another to check it.

```text
request → check source and authority → draft → review → start time
        → test in stages → publish → watch → keep old version and rollback
```

Never use an LLM or a scraped web page as the source of truth for inventory or price.

### 19.18 WF-19 — reconciliation

Run these checks each day during a pilot. After the pilot, choose the frequency based on risk.

| Reconciliation | Equation/check | Exception example |
|---|---|---|
| Source capture | Supported source events = received + documented rejects | The provider shows 101 leads, but intake has 99 |
| CRM persistence | Valid unique events = persisted + open exceptions | A valid event is missing from the CRM |
| Assignment | Open actionable leads = owned + monitored fallback | A lead has no owner |
| Message | Intended eligible sends = sent + suppressed + failed/queued | A message has no final state |
| Visit | Match confirmed/booked IDs in the CRM, calendar and site roster | The calendar event was deleted, but the CRM says confirmed |
| Opt-out | Stop events = suppressions propagated + open exception | The CRM suppresses it, but the campaign list is still active |

For each source, the contract must define its time zone and row or event ID that cannot change. It must also define the extraction start and end, high-water mark, allowed provider delay, overlap for late arrivals, and whether updates may use an earlier date. A repeat run over an overlap window must be idempotent. Save each mismatched row ID, who may repair it, the repair action, the run version and the SLA for how long it may stay open. Never move a watermark beyond a gap that nobody explained. Store the counts, filters and query or export proof. Screenshots alone are not enough.

### 19.19 Workflow versioning and release notes

For every change, write down:

- the request or ticket and the business reason;
- the old and new rule, schema, template or mapping version;
- the risk class and approvers;
- the affected sources, projects and channels;
- the test cases and results;
- the release time and operator;
- the monitoring window and rollback trigger;
- the result after release.

Document an emergency fix after the fact within one business day. Do not let “emergency” become the normal way to avoid controls.

<a id="messaging"></a>

## 20. Conversation and message design

### 20.1 Conversation objective

The goal is not a long chat with a bot. The goal is to complete the next useful action with as little effort as possible. Keep the message true, respect permission and show a clear way to reach a person.

### 20.2 Message-design principles

| Principle | Operating rule |
|---|---|
| Identity | Name the client brand and the related project or context early |
| Relevance | Connect the reply to the enquiry; do not send a general dump of inventory |
| Brevity | Give each message one purpose and one main question or action |
| Truth | Use only current facts that were approved; when unsure, say “a sales adviser will confirm” |
| Choice | Give useful choices, plus `Other / Talk to a person / Stop` |
| Accessibility | Use plain words, short lines, clear dates and times, and readable maps |
| Language | Ask which language the person wants; use checked translations; do not guess from a name |
| Timing | Follow channel rules, the customer's choices and reasonable hours |
| Control | Make it easy to correct, reschedule, cancel or opt out |
| Continuity | Show the earlier context to the person who takes over; do not make the customer repeat everything |
| Data minimisation | Ask only for data that changes the service or routing |
| No manipulation | Do not use fake scarcity, a fake countdown, a hidden bot or an invented claim such as “people viewing now” |

### 20.3 Content governance table

| Content type | Source of truth | Approver | Review trigger |
|---|---|---|---|
| Project name/address/directions | Project master that the client approved | Project/legal owner | The address or site entrance changes |
| Registration/approval references | Client or legal record that is the source of truth, plus a public regulator check | Client legal/compliance | The status or version changes |
| Price/payment plan | Approved price sheet with a date | Sales/commercial head | Every update or expiry |
| Inventory/availability | The client's inventory system | Sales operations | A real-time or set refresh |
| Amenities/specifications | Approved brochure or fact sheet | Project owner | The supporting material changes |
| Offers/deadlines | Written approval of the business terms | Commercial + legal | An expiry or event |
| Visit slots/closures | Roster or calendar that is the source of truth | Site manager | A shift, holiday or event |
| Privacy/consent language | Client privacy or legal owner | Designated approver | A law, purpose or vendor changes |
| WhatsApp template | Platform-approved template plus client approval | Marketing/compliance | A template, category or policy changes |

When practical, every fact that can change needs `value`, `source`, `approved_by`, `approved_at`, `valid_from`, `valid_until/refresh rule` and `version`.

### 20.4 Conversation-state model

```text
NEW_INBOUND
  ├─ needs_identity_or_context → CLARIFY_CONTEXT
  ├─ eligible + simple request → ANSWER_AND_QUALIFY
  ├─ human requested/risk      → HUMAN_HANDOFF
  └─ stop/complaint            → SUPPRESSED_OR_ESCALATED

ANSWER_AND_QUALIFY → SUMMARY → VISIT_PROPOSED → BOOKED → CONFIRMED
         ├─ not ready → PERMITTED_FOLLOW_UP_OR_CLOSED
         ├─ mismatch/unknown → HUMAN_REVIEW
         └─ silence → finite, approved follow-up then CLOSED_NO_RESPONSE
```

Keep state in the CRM or workflow system. Do not keep it only in a model's chat memory.

Before choosing free text or a template, run the [WhatsApp policy gate in Section 30.7](#compliance). If a real user started the conversation, limited free-text replies may be allowed inside the current 24-hour service window. A business that starts contact, or sends it outside that window, usually needs a current approved template that matches the purpose. It also needs valid WhatsApp opt-in for the named business. Neither case avoids RERA, telecom, privacy, truth or opt-out controls.

### 20.5 First-response template anatomy

Use this only as a pattern for a draft. It is not a pre-approved WhatsApp template:

> Hi {{first_name_or_neutral_greeting}}, I’m {{agent_or_team}} from {{client_brand}}. I’m contacting you about your {{project_name}} enquiry on {{source/context}}. I can help you with {{approved_next_step}}. Would you like {{option_a}}, {{option_b}}, or a call from our adviser? Reply Stop if you do not want us to message you.

Rules:

- say that the person sent an enquiry only when the source record proves it;
- do not personalise the message when the identity is unclear or people may share the phone;
- do not show a budget or project interest in a lock-screen preview unless needed;
- even a platform-approved template must still meet permission, purpose and current-policy rules;
- “delivered” does not mean “contacted.”

### 20.6 Qualification sequence example

| Turn | Suggested purpose | Example | Branch |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | Check the intent and context | “Would you like details, a call back, or a site visit?” | If the person wants a visit now, skip extra questions |
| 2 | Check the project or location fit | “Which area or project would you like to see?” | `Not sure` → offer a person or adviser |
| 3 | Ask for the configuration | “Which type of home are you thinking about?” | Give choices the client approved, plus other |
| 4 | Ask for a budget range if needed | “Which broad budget range should the adviser use?” | `Prefer not to say` is still a valid answer |
| 5 | Ask for the timing | “When do you hope to decide: 0–3, 3–6, 6–12 months, later, or not decided?” | Do not shame someone for a long timeline |
| 6 | Choose the next action | Sum up the answers and ask whether the person wants a visit or call | A person corrects the facts |

Do not force someone to answer every question before getting a visit or a call from a person. The client may choose required fields. However, extra effort can reduce conversion and unfairly exclude people.

### 20.7 Human hand-off message

> I want to give you the right answer. I’m sending the details you shared to {{team/agent}}. {{realistic_response_expectation}}. You can also choose {{call/time option}}.

Never say “connecting now” when nobody is working in the queue. Outside service hours, state the real time when the team reopens. Offer a choice of callback time.

### 20.8 Visit proposal and confirmation messages

**Proposal—not yet booked:**

> We can ask for a visit on {{date_option_1}} or {{date_option_2}} at {{site/project}}. Which date do you prefer? I’ll confirm it after the site team reserves the time.

**Confirmed only after reservation:**

> Your visit is confirmed. Project/site: {{project/site}}. Date: {{date}}. Time: {{time}} {{timezone}}. Booking: {{short_reference}}. Host: {{name/team/contact if approved}}. Map: {{approved_link}}. Reply Change or Cancel if needed. Please do not send sensitive documents here.

**Reminder:**

> This is a reminder for your confirmed visit to {{project/site}} on {{date}} at {{time}}. Reply 1 to Confirm, 2 to Change, 3 to Cancel, or Human to speak with a person. Map: {{approved_link}}.

**No-show recovery:**

> You did not arrive for today's planned visit. If you still want to visit, choose another time or ask for a call. It is okay if your plans changed. Reply Stop to end our messages.

For every message, check that the channel is allowed and handle the content or template as approved. Do not send the full sequence just because someone did not update a CRM status. First check the real status with the site owner.

### 20.9 Language and translation QA

For English, Hindi and Marathi variants:

1. Ask a fluent person who knows Pune and property terms to review each version.
2. Keep legal and project names exact. Do not translate registration references.
3. Translate key consent, price, date and cancellation text back into the first language to check it.
4. Test numbers, currency, lakh/crore notation, AM/PM and 24-hour time.
5. Test replies with mixed scripts, words written in another script and spelling variants.
6. Do not change the language without asking because of a guess about the person's background.
7. Store `language_requested`, `template_language` and the fallback.
8. Offer help from a person when confidence in the meaning is low.

### 20.10 Silence and follow-up policy

The client must approve a matrix with a clear end. Split it by source, purpose, how recent the contact is and channel. A cautious design is:

| Situation | Action |
|---|---|
| Active inbound conversation | Reply inside the allowed service context; do not repeat automated messages |
| Customer promises a time | Create one task or message for that requested time, if allowed |
| No response after acknowledgement | Send a small number of useful follow-ups; then close or pause it instead of chasing every day forever |
| Visit booked | Stop sales-chase messages and use the confirmation or reschedule flow |
| Opt-out/complaint | Stop at once and suppress |
| Stale lead/re-engagement | Require a separate, approved campaign decision; do not treat it as an active service chat |

For each template and source, track `messages per lead` and the response, block, complaint and opt-out rates. If a negative rate rises, treat it as a quality alarm. It is not only a marketing number.

### 20.11 Prohibited or high-risk response patterns

- Do not invent a price, inventory, approval, possession date, return, rental yield or appreciation.
- Do not present legal, investment, tax, lending or eligibility advice as a fact.
- Do not steer people in a discriminatory way. Do not collect religion, caste, health, exact finances or family status without a valid need.
- Do not ask for an OTP, password, payment card, bank statement or identity document in normal chat.
- Do not suggest that you are officially linked to the government or regulator.
- Do not hide the automation when the user asks. Do not block access to a person.
- Do not continue after a stop request or complaint.
- Do not create fake scarcity or a fake appointment.
- Do not send the broker's or developer's internal notes to the lead.
- Do not put personal data in group messages, URLs, analytics labels or logs.

### 20.12 Conversation QA scorecard

Score a sample each week. Any critical error fails the conversation, no matter what its total score is.

| Dimension | Weight | Critical failure example |
|---|---:|---|
| Correct identity/context | 15 | Reveals the wrong project or person |
| Factual accuracy/freshness | 20 | Invents a price or availability |
| Permission/suppression | 20 | Sends a message after an opt-out |
| Useful next action | 10 | Gives no path forward or claims a fake handoff |
| Human escalation | 10 | Lets automation handle a risky question alone |
| Data minimisation | 10 | Asks for sensitive data that is not needed |
| Tone/language/accessibility | 5 | Uses words that are impossible to understand or force a choice |
| CRM/audit completeness | 10 | Does not record the interaction or decision |

Suggested internal gate: score at least 90/100 and have no critical failures before sending more traffic. This is an operations target. It is not a standard for the whole industry.

<a id="qualification"></a>

## 21. Lead qualification, scoring and routing

### 21.1 Qualification is triage, not human value

Qualification uses clear business facts to choose which service action comes next. It must not judge a person's value. It must not pretend to be certain or discriminate unlawfully. A lead with low priority still needs a clear result, follow-up that respects consent and a way to correct errors.

### 21.2 Minimum viable qualification schema

| Field | Suggested controlled values | Why it may matter | Safe handling |
|---|---|---|---|
| Project/area interest | Approved project/area/undecided | Helps choose routing and useful content | Keep more than one interest |
| Configuration | Client inventory values/other/unknown | Helps match a product | Never guess it from family size |
| Broad budget range | Approved bands/prefer not to say/unknown | Helps match a product | Keep it optional unless the client proves it is needed |
| Purchase timeline | 0–3/3–6/6–12/>12 months/unknown | Helps set follow-up urgency | Let the customer change it |
| Visit intent | Ready/exploring/callback first/not now | Helps choose the next action | Give a direct visit request priority |
| Preferred visit/call window | Structured time + timezone | Helps schedule the action | Confirm it; do not assume it |
| Financing support requested | Yes/no/unsure | Sends the request to a person | Do not promise or advise on lending |
| Current city/area | Broad value only if operationally needed | Helps plan the visit | Do not ask for an exact home address |
| Language/channel preference | Explicit options | Helps provide the service | Do not guess personal background |
| Agent notes | Short summary of facts | Keeps context when another person takes over | Keep opinions separate from what the customer said |

### 21.3 What not to use for automated priority

Do not use religion, caste, race, health, disability, political views, sexual orientation, surname proxies, exact neighbourhood wealth proxies, family status or other sensitive or protected traits. Use one only if a lawyer or the client's compliance owner documents why the use is lawful and needed. Do not secretly use device type, accent, grammar or language to guess what someone can afford.

### 21.4 Example transparent score

This is only a starting example for a workshop. It is not a model for every client or a promise.

| Signal | Points | Evidence required |
|---|---:|---|
| Clearly asks for a site visit | +30 | Saved customer choice |
| Asks a person to call back within 24 hours | +20 | The time requested |
| Project/configuration matches the current approved offer | +15 | Current master data and the person's stated choice |
| Timeline is 0–3 months | +15 | Customer's answer |
| Budget band overlaps the approved offer | +10 | Optional answer from the customer |
| Gave a meaningful reply in the current conversation | +10 | Inbound event |
| Existing complaint or opt-out is still open | **Suppress, not score** | Compliance state |
| Duplicate/replay or invalid contact | **Treat as an exception, not a score** | Data rule |
| Unknown answers | 0 | Do not make them negative by default |

Example bands:

- `P0 urgent`: there is a complaint, legal risk or service failure. Send it to a specialist instead of treating it as a sales priority.
- `P1 hot`: the score is 50+ or the person clearly wants an immediate visit or call. Alert a person.
- `P2 active`: the score is 20–49. Use the normal response queue.
- `P3 nurture/undecided`: the score is 0–19. Send only follow-up that is allowed and useful.
- `PX`: data is missing or conflicts. Ask a person to review it.

### 21.5 Score governance

- Store the points for each part and the rule version. Do not store only the total.
- Limit related signals so one action does not count three times.
- Keep the intent or fit score separate from permission to communicate and service-risk priority.
- Test against past data only after you fix the result definitions and data leakage.
- Compare conversion and service quality across bands. Do not assume that a link between two facts proves that one caused the other.
- Review false negatives. These are leads with a low score that later booked or attended.
- Check the distribution and results for proxy discrimination that has no valid reason.
- Let authorised staff correct the inputs. Record why they changed them.
- Remove features that are no longer useful. Keep versions of the thresholds.

### 21.6 Routing matrix template

| Priority | Project/site | Customer preference | Working state | Destination | Acceptance target | Fallback |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| P0 service risk | Any | Any | Any | Manager or compliance queue | Urgent target set by client | Escalate to sponsor |
| P1 explicit visit | Project A | Marathi | Open | Eligible Marathi-speaking rep for Project A | Number of minutes set by client | Site-visit coordinator |
| P1 callback | Any | Asked for 18:30 | Future time | Schedule a task for the assigned rep | At the requested time | Manager if nobody is available |
| P2 active | Project B | English | Open | Eligible pool using round robin | Standard SLA | Central queue that someone monitors |
| Any | Mapping is unknown | Any | Any | Mapping exception | Set review target | Do not send a project-specific message |
| Any | Opted out | Channel suppressed | Any | Do not take an outbound action | At once | Privacy owner if the status is disputed |

### 21.7 Fair round-robin

First define which reps are eligible. Then use a transaction-safe cursor or the CRM's supported assignment tool. Record every rep you skip and the reason, such as inactive, on leave, no capacity or not approved for the project. Do not reset counts by hand to favour some staff. A manager who overrides the system must give a reason and create an audit event.

### 21.8 Capacity and presence

| Control | Rule |
|---|---|
| Roster source | Use an approved list owned by the client, with start and end dates |
| Presence | Use available, unavailable, on leave or off shift; choose a safe default when the status is old |
| Open-lead cap | Set it for each rep or team; send extra leads to the fallback |
| Acceptance | You may require a clear accept or reject, with a reason and timer |
| Reassignment | Keep the assignment history and context from any active conversation |
| Departing user | Turn off access; reassign open tasks and leads; keep the identity for audit |
| After hours | Give an honest acknowledgement when allowed, schedule the next action for staffed hours and alert only for a true emergency |

### 21.9 Qualification call/chat summary format

```text
Facts stated by the customer:
- Project/area:
- Configuration:
- Budget band / not shared:
- Timeline:
- Visit/callback preference:

Missing facts or conflicts:
-

Calculated by the system (labelled and easy to explain):
- Priority band and components:

Permission and channel status:
-

Next action, owner, due time:
-
```

Never let a summary made by AI replace the original conversation or structured facts without review. Mark every inference and unclear point.

### 21.10 Measuring qualification quality

| Metric | Definition |
|---|---|
| Completion rate | Enquiries with an allowed result / valid enquiries that enter qualification |
| Structured completeness | Required client fields filled in or clearly marked unknown / fields that apply |
| Correction rate | Records corrected after the summary or fields were created / records reviewed |
| Time to disposition | Median and P90 from valid receipt to an approved qualification result |
| Handoff acceptance | Hot handoffs accepted by a person / hot handoffs sent |
| Visit conversion by band | Unique booked visits / eligible leads in a fixed score band |
| False-negative proxy | Low-band leads that later booked or attended / low-band leads |
| Abandonment point | People who leave or stop replying after each question / people who reach that turn |

Report the band definitions and denominator. After rules change, do not compare months unless you recalculate the old data or clearly mark the break.
