<a id="incident-response"></a>

## 28. Monitoring and incident response

### 28.1 Monitoring layers

| Layer | Signal | Example alert |
|---|---|---|
| Source | Latest event, counts that match, login or authorisation, and data shape | No events during a normally busy time, or source and CRM counts differ |
| Workflow | Success, failure, delay, retries and backlog | Failure rate is high or one queue item is too old |
| CRM | Create, update, routing and API limits | A valid lead has no owner, or a permission error occurs |
| Messaging/telephony | Delivery, failure, quality and cost | Failures suddenly rise, quality warning appears or volume is unusual |
| Booking | Capacity, collisions and CRM-calendar match | The CRM says confirmed but the calendar has no event |
| Data quality | Required fields, impossible state changes and duplicates | A visit is marked attended before its planned start |
| Security/privacy | Login, access, exports, secrets and recipients | A new admin appears, a large export occurs or data may reach the wrong person |
| Business | Slow SLA tail, opt-outs, complaints and visits with no result | Complaints or no-shows suddenly rise |
| Vendor | Status, changes and features being ended | API version will close or the vendor is down |

Check both limits and missing signals. A pipeline that no longer reports errors may itself have stopped working.

Run a planned, approved test journey through the whole path. Use this path: a separate test entry and account mapping → event ledger → test CRM record → assignment → sandbox or non-customer send intent → test visit and calendar path. Clearly mark every made-up test object. Remove it from business measures and block any real outreach. Alert if a check point is late or missing. Delete the test data under the test-retention rule. A real source may simply be quiet, so “time since last event” is unclear. A regular test proves whether the pipeline is quiet or broken.

### 28.2 Alert design

Every alert that needs action must name the client, environment and severity. It must show the detection time and affected workflow and project. It must also give the number and age of items, correlation or sample IDs, likely runbook, owner and way to acknowledge it. Never put a full message, phone number, login secret or unneeded personal data in an alert.

### 28.3 Severity matrix

| Severity | Definition/examples | Initial action |
|---|---|---|
| SEV-1 critical | Data is actively crossing clients or reaching a wrong person; many unauthorised messages; serious account break-in; false confirmations across the system; or leads being destroyed | Turn off the affected real-world action at once. Alert the named owners, keep evidence and open the incident channel |
| SEV-2 high | A major source or CRM is down; many valid leads are not processed; opt-outs do not reach all systems; or booking sync fails and affects customers | Contain it and start the fallback quickly. Tell the client as the contract requires |
| SEV-3 medium | A limited failure has a workaround; a noncritical report is wrong; or one mapping is wrong | Open a ticket, name the owner and target date, and watch how far it spreads |
| SEV-4 low | A visual, document or nonurgent improvement | Put it in the backlog for a planned release |

Your contract may set different times. Never offer an SLA unless someone can be available to meet it.

### 28.4 Incident roles

| Role | Responsibility |
|---|---|
| Incident commander | Sets priorities, makes decisions and sets update times. This person does not fix every technical issue |
| Technical lead | Finds the cause, contains the problem, restores service and keeps evidence |
| Client operational owner | Runs the manual fallback and coordinates staff and sites |
| Communications owner | Sends approved notices to the client and other affected people |
| Privacy/security/legal owner | Judges a breach, notices and regulator duties, and tells the team what evidence to keep |
| Scribe | Records the timeline, possible causes, actions, IDs and decisions |

In a small job, one person may do several of these roles. Name each role before launch.

### 28.5 Incident lifecycle

```text
detect → validate → declare/severity → contain → assess scope
      → communicate → eradicate/fix → recover gradually → reconcile
      → close with approval → post-incident review → preventive actions
```

### 28.6 First 15-minute checklist for a serious incident

1. Confirm that the problem is in production. Record when it first happened, if known, and when it was found.
2. Open an incident record. Name the commander and scribe.
3. Find the unsafe real-world action. Turn off only the affected message, booking or connector path.
4. Keep logs, setup and version information. Do not destroy evidence.
5. If it is safe, continue receiving incoming leads and send them to a manual queue.
6. Check whether the issue includes personal data, wrong recipients, opt-outs or account secrets.
7. Find every affected client, project, channel and record. Check whether harm is still happening.
8. Tell the required internal and client roles through the agreed route.
9. Set the time for the next update.
10. Do not guess the root cause or send a large group a correction without review.

### 28.7 Containment playbooks

| Incident | Immediate containment | Recovery prerequisite |
|---|---|---|
| Duplicate outbound messages | Turn off sending, keep receiving leads and stop repeats waiting in the queue | Fix idempotency, compare all affected records and test a replay |
| Wrong project/person message | Stop the affected template or mapping and keep the evidence | Fix the mapping or identity check. Complete client and privacy review and pass the test set |
| Opt-out ignored | Stop the related campaigns and queues. Force suppression at the safest layer | Make all systems agree and check every affected person |
| Lead source not arriving | Start the approved fallback, export or manual intake | Fix the cause, find the missing time period and import it safely |
| CRM outage | Within approved limits, queue only the minimum event references. Tell the client | When service returns, replay in a controlled way and remove duplicates |
| Calendar mismatch | Stop confirmations, mark affected visits and have the coordinator check them | Make CRM and calendar agree, then give customers correct information |
| Credential compromise | Turn off the identity or rotate the secret. Review sessions and logs | Restore least access and judge the full affected scope |
| AI unsupported claim | Turn off the AI reply or action. Send the case to a person or fixed rules | Fix the knowledge, prompt or control and pass all repeat tests |
| Mass-cost/retry loop | Turn off the trigger or retry and limit system and provider spending | Understand the cause and backlog, then replay slowly and safely |
| Dashboard wrong | Mark it unavailable and stop using it for decisions | Fix the query or meaning and tell users how past reports were affected |

### 28.8 Wrong-recipient response

Do not automatically send an apology that repeats the private information. Stop the faulty path. Find the data or content, recipients, time, delivery and read evidence, and continuing risk. Bring in the client's privacy and legal owner. Follow that owner's approved decision about messages and notices. Fix identity and routing, test similar cases, and list affected records. Where possible, record deletion and containment. The automation builder must not decide legal notification duties alone.

### 28.9 Manual fallback

Prepare a minimum fallback that the client has approved:

```text
Source/export or fallback notification
→ controlled shared/client queue
→ named manager assigns
→ rep logs attempt/outcome using minimal form
→ coordinator confirms visits manually
→ later import/reconcile using stable IDs
```

The fallback must state who can access it, which fields it uses, how often work is done, how duplicates are stopped and how long data is kept. It must also state the volume at which people can no longer handle it safely.

### 28.10 Recovery and replay checklist

- You know the root cause, or you have a safe workaround.
- A reviewer checked the affected time period and the query that selects the records.
- Check each current customer, visit and suppression state again.
- A dry run shows the exact actions and counts without making real changes.
- Confirm idempotency and duplicate protection.
- A second approver checks any bulk action that customers will see.
- The replay speed stays inside provider limits.
- Run a small canary group first and make sure its records match.
- Monitoring watches the backlog and every real-world effect.
- The client confirms what is true in the real operation.
- Merge the manual queue without losing or duplicating work.

### 28.11 Incident update template

```text
Incident ID / severity / status
Known start and detection time
Confirmed impact and affected scope
What is NOT known yet
Containment completed
Customer/operational workaround
Current investigation/recovery action and owner
Decisions/support needed from client
Next update time
```

During the incident, do not blame one staff member as the root cause. Do not put personal data in the update. Do not promise a restore time unless evidence supports it.

### 28.12 Post-incident review

Within a reasonable time for the seriousness of the incident, record:

- the effect and length of the incident in simple words;
- the exact timeline;
- how it was found and why detection was early or late;
- the technical, process and company conditions that helped cause it;
- which protections worked and which failed;
- customer, privacy and security reviews and messages;
- proof that service recovered and records now match;
- each corrective action, owner, date and proof of completion;
- similar systems and clients that were checked;
- changes to runbooks, tests and training;
- any contract or SLA follow-up;
- what the team learned, without blame.

The root cause is rarely just “human error.” Ask why one person's mistake was able to cause harm.

### 28.13 Corrective-action priorities

| Priority | Example |
|---|---|
| Prevent recurrence | Idempotency, a permission check and one-step state control |
| Reduce blast radius | Separate clients, use a recipient allow-list, and limit rates and costs |
| Detect earlier | Compare records, use a canary, and alert when states do not match |
| Recover faster | Test the fallback, replay and restore runbook |
| Improve decision | Make severity, RACI and the contact tree clear |
| Prove closure | Pass repeat tests and watch the release. Do not accept only the word “fixed” |

### 28.14 Incident metrics

Track the number of incidents by severity and type. Track the effect on customers and data. Measure time to detect, acknowledge, contain, restore and make all records agree. Also track repeats, late corrective actions, false or noisy alerts, and the percentage found by your team instead of the customer. Do not improve only the average resolution time while hiding a few very serious slow cases.

<a id="security"></a>

# Part IV — Compliance and business operations

## 29. Security, privacy and data retention

### 29.1 Security objective

Keep client systems and personal data confidential, correct and available. Copy and keep as little data as possible. Often, the safest choice is to leave data in the client CRM instead of making another copy.

### 29.2 Shared-responsibility matrix

Assign these duties in every client contract. The labels under Indian law depend on how the work is actually arranged.

| Control | Client default | Your default | Vendor default |
|---|---|---|---|
| Purpose/lawfulness/notice/consent | Make the accountable decision and keep evidence | Apply the written rules and report gaps | Supply platform features and terms |
| Project facts/content | Approve it and keep it current | Use only approved versions | Store and deliver it as configured |
| CRM/WABA/telephony ownership | Own the tenant, business identity and billing | Use service access with least privilege | Secure the platform under the contract |
| User access | Approve each user and role | Give access only to authorised staff; review it | Supply authentication and audit features |
| Workflow design | Approve the business rules | Design, test, monitor and document the workflow | Supply the execution infrastructure |
| Lead/customer response | Staff the human team and give final advice | Automate only the agreed, limited steps | Deliver through the channel |
| Security incident | Make legal and business decisions and send notices | Detect and contain it; inform the client and support evidence work | Notify and help as the terms require |
| Rights/requests | Verify the request and decide the response | Search, export, correct or delete as instructed | Supply technical features to complete the request |
| Retention | Approve a schedule by purpose and category | Configure retention, then delete or return data | Apply available settings and the backup lifecycle |
| Exit | Receive data and configuration; revoke access | Export, hand over and delete; provide an attestation | Supply account export and deletion methods |

### 29.3 Data-flow inventory

Complete one row for each data flow before production starts:

| Field | Required detail |
|---|---|
| Flow ID/purpose | State the exact business purpose and owner |
| Data subjects | List prospects, customers, staff and partners |
| Fields/classification | List only the exact minimum fields; mark higher-risk data |
| Source and collection context | State whether it came from a form, portal, inbound message or human entry |
| Notice/permission/basis evidence | Give the client-approved reference and version |
| Systems/parties | Map source → automation → CRM → channel → analytics/support |
| Processing locations/transfers | List vendor and support locations |
| Access roles | List named role groups and privileged accounts |
| Storage/logs/backups | List every permanent and temporary copy |
| Retention/trigger | State the period or event rule and all exceptions |
| Rights/search key | Explain how to find a verified person in every system |
| Security controls | List encryption, authentication, audit, isolation and monitoring controls |
| Exit/deletion | State the method, owner, proof and treatment of remaining backups |

Draw the real flow. “Cloud” does not name a location or a control.

### 29.4 Data minimisation worksheet

Ask these questions for every proposed field:

1. Which written purpose cannot work without this field?
2. Would a broad range or yes/no value be enough?
3. Can the field stay only in the client system?
4. Does it come from the person, or is it inferred?
5. Could it cause unfair steering or embarrassment if exposed?
6. Who needs the field, and for how long?
7. Can the workflow accept `unknown` instead?
8. Can you keep it out of logs, alerts, analytics and AI prompts?

Keep identity and money secrets out of this service by default. This includes Aadhaar/PAN/passport images, bank/card/UPI credentials and OTPs. Also exclude detailed income statements, health/religion/caste data and precise live location. Exclude unrelated family details and secret authentication data too. A client may truly need a document later in its sales process. In that case, direct the person to the client’s approved secure process. Do not casually put the document into lead automation.

### 29.5 Threat model

| Threat actor/event | Likely path | Impact | Primary controls |
|---|---|---|---|
| Stolen founder/admin credential | Phishing, a reused password or a device | Access, messages or exports across clients | MFA, a password manager, separate identities, least privilege and alerts |
| Malicious/curious staff | Too much CRM or export access | Lead theft or disclosure | Limit roles, fields and exports; audit and offboard |
| Misconfigured workflow | Wrong mapping, tenant or recipient | Disclosure and false messages | Separate environments, tenant-scoped configuration, UAT, a canary and a kill switch |
| Webhook spoof/replay | An exposed endpoint or no signature | Fake leads, actions or costs | Verify signatures; control timestamps and replays; use idempotency and rate limits |
| Prompt injection | A lead or document gives instructions to the AI/tool | Data exfiltration or an unsafe action | Separate untrusted data, allow-list tools, validate output and require human approval |
| Vendor compromise/outage | A subprocessor incident | Lost data, access or availability | Due diligence, data minimisation, contract notice, fallback and exit plans |
| Lost device/export | A local download or unlocked laptop | Large data disclosure | Disk encryption, device lock, no local exports and remote revocation |
| Shared client password | A password in chat/email or a common admin account | No clear attribution and possible compromise | Use a named service user and vault; rotate and review access |
| Cross-client analytics/logging | A shared table, query or alert | Data leaks between tenants | Isolate and test tenants; separate high-risk tenants |
| Retry/cost loop | Logic or provider errors | Spam, extra cost or channel bans | Idempotency, caps, back-off and a circuit breaker |
| Insider lead resale | An export or manual copy | Harm and loss of trust | Contract terms, access/export monitoring, minimum access and enforcement |

### 29.6 Identity and access management

- Use client-owned, named accounts. Do not share an `admin` account;
- Require MFA for email, CRM, automation, cloud, code, messaging and the password manager;
- Use a separate service identity for each client and integration where supported;
- Give each role and field only the access it needs;
- Give elevated access for troubleshooting only for a set time;
- Do not give production access to people who only sell or design;
- Review access every quarter or when risk changes. Offboard people at once;
- Log changes to admins, exports, bulk actions, templates, workflows, owners and permissions;
- Keep the emergency/break-glass account in a vault. Monitor and test it;
- Keep the recovery email and phone under business control, not a departing contractor’s control;
- Ban copies of client data in personal cloud drives, notes or consumer AI tools.

### 29.7 Client separation

Use separate tenants, workspaces, projects, credentials and datasets where possible. If infrastructure is shared:

- Get the fixed `tenant_id` from authenticated configuration, never from customer text;
- Enforce the tenant scope for every query and action;
- Give service identities access to only one client where possible;
- Keep secrets and logs separate;
- Test object IDs and cached context across tenants;
- Use approved aggregation and minimum group sizes for analytics;
- Run backups, exports and restores by tenant;
- Prevent one client from changing another client’s prompt, mapping or webhook;
- Record how far an incident could spread.

### 29.8 Secret management

| Rule | Practice |
|---|---|
| Creation | Use strong provider-issued credentials with the smallest scope and shortest useful life |
| Storage | Use an encrypted secret manager or platform credential store |
| Use | Refer to the secret ID; never paste a secret into workflow text, a log or a prompt |
| Distribution | Grant access automatically or through a controlled process; never use chat, email or a spreadsheet |
| Rotation | Rotate on schedule, after a personnel/vendor incident and after suspected exposure |
| Logging | Hide tokens, authentication headers, cookies, OTPs and connection strings |
| Revocation | Name an owner and test the procedure |
| Backup | Never export plaintext; document the recovery method |

If a secret appears in a ticket, screenshot, repository or chat, treat it as compromised. Rotate it. Deleting the message is not enough.

### 29.9 Endpoint, device and work practices

- Use a supported OS and browser. Install security updates promptly;
- Use full-disk encryption, automatic screen lock and a device password or biometric;
- Use a separate work account or browser profile for each client when useful;
- Use antivirus or endpoint protection and a firewall;
- Never use a public or shared computer. Avoid untrusted Wi-Fi or use approved protection;
- Turn off unneeded browser extensions and clipboard syncing;
- Keep source code and configuration in a controlled repository. Scan them for secrets;
- Never download production data to a phone or USB drive;
- Encrypt any necessary temporary export. Remove it securely on schedule;
- Lock up printed notes. Avoid printing lead lists;
- Define how to report a lost device and revoke remote sessions and tokens.

### 29.10 Webhook/API security

- Use HTTPS with a valid certificate;
- Check the provider signature/HMAC, or equal authentication, against the raw request as documented;
- Check timestamps and use a replay window where supported;
- Allow-list the method, content type, schema and size;
- Add rate limits and abuse protection;
- Do not use a random, hard-to-guess endpoint instead of authentication;
- Treat all input as untrusted. Prevent injection and unsafe deserialisation;
- Return only a small response. Never expose stack traces or secrets;
- Allow-list outbound destinations. Protect against SSRF and open redirects;
- Use idempotency and keep a durable audit reference;
- Set dependency timeouts, a circuit breaker and bounded retries;
- Keep an inventory of API versions and deprecations;
- Match penetration and security review effort to the custom exposure.

### 29.11 Logging safely

Log the event, not every byte of data.

| Log | Prefer | Avoid |
|---|---|---|
| Intake | Tenant, source, event and correlation IDs; authentication/validation result; timestamps | The full form payload |
| Routing | Rule version, eligible pool IDs, selected owner and reason | A personal profile narrative |
| Messaging | Template and version, purpose, provider ID and status | The full chat by default |
| AI | Use case, model and prompt version; retrieved source IDs; decision or error | Unredacted conversations or prompts that contain secrets |
| Booking | Visit, site, slot and host IDs; state changes | Unneeded travel or family details |
| Admin | Actor, action, object and before/after reference | A password or token |
| Error | Code, dependency, correlation and safe sample reference | A stack trace with payloads or credentials in broad alerts |

Limit and monitor access to logs. Logs are sensitive too. Define how long to keep them and how to search or delete them.

### 29.12 Encryption and key ownership

Require encryption while data moves. Confirm that each vendor encrypts stored data and backups and manages keys for the level of risk. For high-risk or custom systems, document the key owner, access, rotation, recovery and separation. Encryption does not fix excessive collection, broad permissions or a message sent to the wrong person.

### 29.13 Retention schedule template

Do not copy these example periods without review. The client or legal owner must approve a schedule based on purpose and law.

| Data class | System | Purpose | Retention trigger/period | Deletion method | Exception/hold | Owner |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Raw intake payload |  | Check and reconcile data | Keep it for the shortest practical time after reconciliation | Delete it automatically | Incident or legal hold |  |
| CRM lead/contact |  | Sales and customer service | Follow the client-approved lifecycle rule | Delete or anonymise it in the CRM | Transaction or legal record |  |
| Consent/suppression evidence |  | Prove and honour the person’s choice | Keep it only as long as needed; minimum suppression data may remain after the marketing profile | Minimise or anonymise it | Dispute or legal need |  |
| Message/call metadata |  | Service and audit | Keep it for the approved period | Delete it from the provider and CRM | Complaint or contract need |  |
| Recordings/transcripts |  | A specific, approved QA purpose | Keep them for a short, stated period | Purge them securely | Investigation |  |
| Automation/security execution logs |  | Debugging, audit, security and incident response | Under current CERT-In scope, keep a rolling minimum of 180 days in India. From 13 May 2027, use the counsel-approved one-year retention under DPDP Rules 6/8 for relevant logs/personal data | Keep them in a restricted India-resident archive, then purge them securely | Incident or legal hold | Security/privacy owner |
| Analytics |  | Improve the funnel | Prefer de-identified or aggregated data | Aggregate or delete it | — |  |
| Backups |  | Recovery | Use a rotating schedule | Let them expire or use cryptographic erasure where supported | Legal or incident need |  |
| Your sales prospect data |  | B2B relationship | Follow a review, expiry and opt-out rule | Remove it from the CRM or suppress it | Contract or tax need |  |

### 29.14 Deletion workflow

1. Verify the request and authority through the client’s approved method. Do not collect more identity proof than needed.
2. Open a restricted ticket. Pause unrelated automated outreach.
3. Confirm the scope, systems, purposes and legal or contract exceptions. Decide whether suppression evidence must remain.
4. Use approved identifiers to search source copies, the CRM, messaging, automation, analytics, tickets, exports and vendors.
5. Get the client’s decision or instruction when you act for the client.
6. Delete, anonymise, correct or restrict data as instructed. Never damage other customers’ records.
7. Record the result for each system, any remaining backups and their planned expiry.
8. Ask the accountable client to communicate with the person.
9. Keep the minimum suppression control needed to prevent re-import or new contact.
10. Close the request only when you have evidence, not a verbal promise.

### 29.15 Data export and access requests

For every export, verify the requester and get client approval. Use the smallest scope, a secure format and channel, suitable encryption, an expiry time and a download audit. Before release, check for another person’s or client’s data. Never send a raw database or spreadsheet through personal WhatsApp.

### 29.16 Vendor due diligence

| Area | Evidence/question |
|---|---|
| Corporate/product | Identify the legal entity, service, support and financial viability |
| Security | Check MFA/RBAC, encryption, testing/assurance, vulnerability handling and the incident process |
| Privacy/data | Check roles, purposes, locations, subprocessors, retention, deletion and support for rights |
| Contract | Check confidentiality, breach notice, help, liability, audit/evidence and termination terms |
| Reliability | Check architecture and status history, backups, recovery, rate limits and support tiers |
| Product change | Check notice of API, version and deprecation changes, and check export options |
| AI | Check training use, retention, models, safety and human controls |
| Commercial | Check the full pricing unit, overage, currency, tax, lock-in and portability |

The client or accountable owner must accept risk. The implementer must not accept it silently for them.

### 29.17 Secure contractor/employee onboarding

- Get signed duties for confidentiality, IP and data;
- Use lawful background and reference checks that match the person’s access;
- Give security, privacy and client-specific training;
- Give a named account with least privilege. Use a managed device where the risk calls for one;
- Require work in a test environment before production;
- Do not allow subcontracting or data access beyond the contract;
- Review activity and support quality;
- Use an offboarding checklist. Revoke access, recover assets and get a deletion attestation.

### 29.18 Security review before each new source/project

Ask whether it adds anything new. Check data fields, people, purpose, jurisdiction, vendor, public endpoint and credential. Also check message/template, AI use, retention, user role, volume/cost, availability dependency and regulated property activity. Any `yes` requires the related data-flow, threat, contract and test update.

### 29.19 Minimum security baseline before first pilot

- Use a separate business email and domain, plus a password manager;
- Turn on MFA for every core account;
- Keep production tenants and billing in the client’s name;
- Use named service identities with least privilege;
- Sign terms for confidentiality, scope and data processing;
- Keep data-flow and access registers;
- Use an encrypted, supported laptop with lock and update controls;
- Never use real data in a demo or upload it to a personal spreadsheet or AI tool;
- Use signed webhook/API authentication and idempotency;
- Redact logs, set retention and restrict access;
- Test tenant separation;
- Keep backups and configuration exports. Drill restore and fallback steps;
- Keep an alert and incident contact tree and a kill switch;
- Test opt-outs, wrong recipients and credential compromise;
- Define offboarding and end-of-contract deletion or return.

<a id="compliance"></a>

## 30. India compliance: RERA, WhatsApp, TRAI, DPDP and CERT-In

### 30.1 Launch-blocking conclusion

This service faces a real risk of being classified under RERA. Section 2(zm) of the Real Estate (Regulation and Development) Act applies to a paid person who introduces possible buyers and sellers. It says this can happen “through any medium.” Section 9 says an unregistered real-estate agent cannot help with a sale or purchase in a registered project. A paid vendor may fit this wording if the vendor, or its automation, responds to prospects, qualifies them and schedules them. Payment by a retainer instead of commission may not change that result. No official ruling was found that directly covers this exact managed-automation model. Applying the law here is therefore a legal inference, not a final court or regulator decision. Get a written Maharashtra RERA opinion before any live work that faces buyers. [RERA Act, sections 2(zm), 9 and 10](https://www.indiacode.nic.in/indiacode/bitstream/123456789/2158/1/A201616.pdf).

Section 62 sets a penalty for an agent’s failure to register or breach of section 9 or 10. The penalty is ₹10,000 for each day of default, up to a total of 5% of the relevant property cost. Because of this penalty, do not run informal tests on real customer leads.

### 30.2 Choose one operating model before selling

| Model | What you may do | What you must not do | RERA posture |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A. Backend RevOps implementation** | Set up the client-owned CRM, routing, dashboards, reliability controls and internal tasks. Train staff and monitor system health | Vendor staff and models must not message or call buyers, recommend property, introduce parties, schedule visits or earn fees linked to a sale | This gives a much stronger argument that you are not an agent. Still get written counsel advice about the exact facts |
| **B. Registered buyer-facing VisitOps** | With written promoter/client authority, run an approved response, qualification and site-visit workflow within strict limits | Do not cover an unapproved project. Do not negotiate, make a binding promise, take booking money, give legal/finance/investment advice or invent a fact | Do not operate until current MahaRERA agent registration, competency certification, association with the exact project and all applicable advertising, record and reporting controls are complete |

If counsel does not clearly approve Model A as you will run it, use Model B before any buyer contact. A contract label such as “software consultant” cannot override what you actually do.

### 30.3 Non-negotiable professional sign-offs

| Priority | Written question |
|---:|---|
| 1 | Ask a Maharashtra RERA lawyer: Does this exact SOW, software behaviour and payment make the founder/entity an agent? If not, define what the business may and may not do. |
| 2 | Ask a RERA lawyer: How must the project and agent registration numbers, QR and disclosures appear in one-to-one WhatsApp, SMS, chat, landing pages and voice? |
| 3 | Ask a telecom/TSP specialist: Which DLT/140 route, consent scrub and auto-dialler notice applies to manual, click-to-call, predictive and AI voice? |
| 4 | Ask a privacy/IT lawyer: What current SPDI duties apply? Confirm DPDP roles, vendors/transfers, Rule 8 retention, recording and breach readiness for May 2027. |
| 5 | Ask an AML lawyer/CA: Do the agent classification and turnover trigger PMLA reporting-entity duties? |
| 6 | Ask a CA: Confirm the GST SAC/rate, intermediary/export/imported SaaS treatment, TDS, books/ITR, advance tax and PTEC/PTRC. |
| 7 | Ask an employment lawyer/HR: Confirm rules for an outside business, conflicts, confidentiality, non-solicitation, IP and an NOC. |

### 30.4 RERA operating obligations

The national Act sets duties for agents. They must not help with unregistered projects. They must keep the required books and records. They must avoid unfair trade practices and false claims about affiliation or facts. They must also help an allottee obtain the information and documents due to them. Relevant Maharashtra orders add local steps. Use [MahaRERA's agent guidance](https://www.maharera.maharashtra.gov.in/guidance-for-agents), and check current orders before each project.

| Control | Operational action |
|---|---|
| Agent status | Register the correct entity or person. Store the number, scope and expiry |
| Competency | Before buyer contact, the founder and every staff member who talks with homebuyers must hold the required current competency certificate |
| Project registration | Check the exact phase, promoter, live status, validity or extension and official URL. A similar project name is not enough |
| Promoter association | Before any facilitation, the promoter’s disclosure or update must name the founder/entity as agent. It must cover the exact project and phase under RERA section 4(2)(j) and the applicable MahaRERA order. Keep portal evidence. Written commercial authority alone is not enough |
| Records/reporting | Keep separate records for each project. Name an owner and calendar MahaRERA filings, such as applicable Form 6 |
| Content | Use only approved, current facts for the project and phase. Show agent/project numbers and the QR/link where required |
| Conduct | Do not make false claims about standards, approvals or affiliations. Avoid unfair practices, pressure and misleading comparisons |
| Money/authority | Under this service, do not take deposits, make an allotment, negotiate or bind the promoter |
| Renewal/termination | Stop the workflow before the registration, association or project authority expires |

The compliance research found that MahaRERA Order 43/2023 took effect on 1 April 2023. It requires disclosure of project association, separate project records and Form 6 reporting every half year. It lists the April–September report as due on 20 October. It lists the October–March report as due on 20 April. Ask counsel to confirm the current version and how it applies, then add it to the calendar: [MahaRERA Order 43/2023](https://maharera.maharashtra.gov.in/order-no-432023).

MahaRERA orders have also required competency certification since 1 January 2024. This applies to agents and relevant employees who deal with homebuyers. Check the approved training, exam and current transition rules directly: [Order 41A](https://maharera.maharashtra.gov.in/sites/default/files/Orders_and_circulars/order_41A.pdf) and [agent-registration highlights](https://www.maharera.maharashtra.gov.in/index.php/highlights-of-maharera-agent-registration-detail).

### 30.5 Project activation checklist

Do not turn on a source until one unchangeable campaign manifest contains all of these items:

```text
Client/promoter legal name and the signatory who gives authority
Operating-model decision and counsel memo; for Model B, current entity/agent registration and competency certificates for every buyer-facing person
Exact name of the project and phase
Project registration number, official page, status and validity date
Evidence of the promoter-agent association
Approved QR asset and the date it was checked
IDs for the lead source, form, ad and campaign
Exact consent/notice text, channels, purpose and version
Approved source, owner and expiry for price, inventory and claims
Message and call scripts/templates, plus platform/DLT IDs
Audience, source, date range and frequency cap
Human, project, legal and privacy approvers
Kill switch, incident contact and retention rule
```

The workflow preflight must stop activity when registration, authority, content, consent or template validity expires.

### 30.6 Real-estate advertising and claims

RERA gives “advertisement” a broad meaning across media. Maharashtra Rule 14(2) applies when a registered agent issues a promotion for a specific project. It requires the agent registration number and the project registration number. MahaRERA Order 46B is dated 21 August 2023. It extends the project QR requirement to covered promotions and advertisements by agents. The QR must be easy to read and scan. It must appear with the project registration number and website. Also follow the extra display rules in Order 46C dated 8 April 2025. Counsel still needs to decide how the rules apply to one-to-one plain text and voice in each medium. Graphical, web and social advertisements are clearly covered cases and are not optional. Ask counsel to check the current orders and exact medium. Use the [MahaRERA Order 46/2023 starting document](https://maharera.maharashtra.gov.in/sites/default/files/Orders_and_circulars/115.pdf), [official 46B enforcement decision](https://maharera.maharashtra.gov.in/sites/default/files/final_order_document/Konkan_FO_144of2024_0.pdf) and [46C enforcement decision](https://maharera.maharashtra.gov.in/sites/default/files/final_order_document/ord_Pune_36OF2O25.pdf). Consumer-protection rules also ban misleading advertising: [CCPA misleading-advertisement guidelines](https://consumeraffairs.nic.in/latestnews/guidelines-prevention-misleading-advertisements-and-endorsements-misleading).

| Claim | Automation rule |
|---|---|
| Price/payment plan | Get it from a dated, approved source. Show all conditions. Make it expire automatically |
| Inventory/“last unit” | Do not make the claim without a current authoritative source. Otherwise, require human confirmation |
| Possession/completion | Use the exact approved or RERA-supported statement. Include the date context |
| Approval/registration | Name the exact phase and authority reference. Do not imply approval covers everything |
| Area | Use the legal or client-approved carpet or other definition. Label it clearly |
| Appreciation/return/rent | Do not invent or guarantee it. Send the question to a qualified human for a legally approved response |
| Loan/eligibility | Make no promise. Only a lender or client specialist may answer |
| Amenities/specifications | Use approved material for the exact phase. State correctly whether each item is proposed or complete |
| Scarcity/deadline | Require real written commercial evidence. Set an automatic expiry |
| Testimonial/award | Get permission. State an exact scope and date that can be checked |

### 30.7 WhatsApp policy gate

WhatsApp has its own private platform contract on top of Indian law. It does not replace TRAI, privacy, RERA or consumer rules. As of 3 September 2026, a business may contact a person on WhatsApp only if the person gave the mobile number and opted in. The opt-in must clearly say that later messages will come through WhatsApp. It must also name the business that will send them. Consent for another channel, business or brand cannot be reused automatically. The business must honour opt-outs. It generally must use approved Message Templates to start conversations. Free-form replies are allowed only during the 24 hours after the user’s last message. Any automated experience in that window needs a fast, clear and direct path to a human. Check again before launch: [WhatsApp Business Messaging Policy](https://whatsappbusiness.com/policy/).

| Situation | Allowed system behaviour |
|---|---|
| Number is present, but WhatsApp permission is absent or unknown | Do not send proactive WhatsApp messages. Create a review task or an eligible alternative-channel task |
| User messages the business | Reply within the current 24-hour customer-service window. The reply must still follow the stated purpose, law and human-escalation rule |
| Business wants to start a conversation or the window is closed | Use only an approved template that matches the purpose, with valid permission and current policy |
| User says stop anywhere | Cancel queued messages and apply suppression everywhere. Do not wait for one exact keyword |
| Meta approved the template | Still require client approval, permission, truth and compliance with RERA and Indian law |
| Client wants one vendor number shared by many brands | Reject unclear identity. Default to a client-owned, verified WABA and number |
| Unofficial browser or bulk tool | Reject it. Use the official platform or BSP |

WhatsApp pricing depends on the category of the delivered message and the recipient’s market. It changes over time. Keep the WABA/BSP and usage in the client’s name, or pass the cost through clearly. Never promise that a rate will stay the same: [official pricing](https://whatsappbusiness.com/products/platform-pricing/).

### 30.8 Consent record for WhatsApp

Store the named business or brand, project and purpose, channel, affirmative action, exact notice or checkbox version, source or form URL/ID, timestamp, scope and evidence reference. Also store the withdrawal method, status and time, plus the result of applying it across systems. Generic portal terms do not count automatically as WhatsApp opt-in. Having a phone number or DLT record does not count automatically either.

Use this cautious checkbox draft only after lawyer, client and platform approval:

> By ticking this box, I agree that **[developer legal name/brand]** may contact me on WhatsApp about **[project/phase]** and arrange a site visit. The contact may come through its authorised real-estate agent, **[agent legal name and MahaRERA number, if applicable]**. I can reply STOP at any time. [Privacy notice].

Where required, use a separate unticked choice for each promotional channel and purpose. Do not make bundled consent a condition for an unrelated service.

### 30.9 TRAI/DLT/SMS gate

India’s commercial-communications rules require registration of the sender or principal entity. They also require registered headers, content and consent templates, the relevant registered telemarketer/access-provider chain and list scrubbing. The current consolidated TCCCPR text puts real estate in preference category 2. Promotional messages must use the required revocation and opt-out process. Do not use ordinary personal subscriber resources as an informal commercial channel. See [TRAI advice to senders](https://www.trai.gov.in/advice-to-senders) and the [consolidated TCCCPR text dated 21 May 2026](https://www.trai.gov.in/sites/default/files/2026-05/CA_21052026.pdf).

A website or CRM checkbox may provide privacy or WhatsApp evidence. By itself, it does not create an entry in the DLT Consent Register. If you rely on explicit telecom consent, get and record it through the DCA/DLT process approved by the access provider. Keep the brand, scope, channel, timestamp, status and revocation record. Every promotional SMS must include its required opt-out method. After a person revokes consent, the sender must not start trying to get that consent again for 90 days. The person may choose to opt in again sooner without being asked. Confirm the steps with the client’s access provider or registered telemarketer because their interfaces and duties differ.

```text
Client/developer acts as the Principal Entity
→ use a registered header/number and registered content/consent templates
→ use the registered telemarketer/CPaaS chain
→ check the target list against consent, preference, time and day rules
→ send with IDs and an audit status
→ apply each revocation or complaint across systems
```

Treat a property pitch or site-visit offer as promotional unless the client’s telecom specialist/TSP gives written reasons to treat it differently. Do not call it “service” just to avoid preference rules. WhatsApp opt-in does not replace DLT/DND compliance. DLT consent does not replace WhatsApp opt-in.

### 30.10 Voice calls and AI diallers

The TRAI guidance found in the current research uses the 140 series for promotional voice calls. It also requires written notice to the originating access provider before using an auto-dialler or robocall. The notice must state its purpose. A direction dated 10 August 2026 covers 1601. At first, it applies to utility and logistics or courier service calls, not real-estate promotions. Before any manual, click-to-call, predictive or AI pilot, get written setup confirmation from the client’s TSP or registered telemarketer. Also complete DND and consent scrubbing: [TRAI sender advice](https://www.trai.gov.in/advice-to-senders), [Second Amendment dated 12 February 2025](https://www.trai.gov.in/sites/default/files/2025-02/Regulation_12022025_0.pdf), [1601 direction](https://trai.gov.in/direction-allocation-and-operationalization-1601-numbering-series-service-and-transactional-voice).

Never scale promotional calls with your ordinary personal ten-digit SIM or a salesperson’s SIM. Do not include automated calls in the first pilot unless specialist approval, caller identity, disclosure, a staffed transfer path, a recording decision and all tests are complete.

### 30.11 Call recording/transcription

This research found no primary legal source that supports a safe, general “one-party consent in India” rule for this commercial service. Electronic-evidence rules do not by themselves allow secret recording. Use this cautious default:

1. Name the business and agent.
2. Tell the person about automation and any synthetic voice.
3. Clearly state why you record or transcribe and how long you keep it.
4. Ask the person to say yes before you capture the main conversation.
5. If the person says no, stop recording and transcription. Offer an unrecorded, text or human option where possible.
6. Limit access and downloads. Keep the data for as little time as possible. Do not train models without separate authority.
7. Get a written decision from telecom and privacy counsel.

Suggested simple draft:

> This call uses a synthetically generated AI voice. Namaste, main **[client]** ka automated assistant hoon. Yeh call aapki **[project/phase]** enquiry ke baare mein hai. Quality aur follow-up ke liye hum call record/transcribe karenge. Kya main continue karun? Aap human adviser se bhi baat kar sakte hain.

The IT Rules amendment dated 20 February 2026 covers synthetically generated information. It may create disclosure and provenance duties if the service is an applicable intermediary. That classification depends on the facts. Disclose the synthetic content in every case, and get advice from IT counsel: [official Gazette amendment](https://www.meity.gov.in/static/uploads/2026/02/f55fe52418b03f58b0669f6a8bc03b6d.pdf).

### 30.12 Privacy law timeline as of 3 September 2026

The DPDP Act and Rules start in stages. Do not wrongly claim that all DPDP operating duties are already in force. Also, do not wait until 2027 to build the controls.

| Date | Act/rules status | Operating treatment |
|---|---|---|
| 13 November 2025 | The notification was published. The listed provisions on institutions, definitions, the Board and related matters started, along with Rules 1, 2 and 17–21 | Track the provisions now in force and the transition law |
| 13 November 2026 | Act section 6(9), section 27(1)(d) and Rule 4 start | These cover Consent Managers. Simply keeping consent records does not automatically make you a registered Consent Manager |
| **13 May 2027** | Core rules for private-sector processing, notice and consent, duties, rights, security and breaches start, along with most remaining operating provisions and rules | Be fully ready for production before this date |

The primary basis is [G.S.R. 843(E), 13 November 2025](https://www.meity.gov.in/static/uploads/2025/11/c56ceae6c383460ca69577428d36828b.pdf) and [final DPDP Rules, G.S.R. 846(E)](https://www.meity.gov.in/static/uploads/2025/11/53450e6e5dc0bfa85ebd78686cadad39.pdf). The dates come from the Gazette’s one-year and eighteen-month stages, not from the date a webpage was uploaded.

### 30.13 Current privacy baseline before May 2027

During the transition, the IT Act/SPDI framework and contract and confidentiality duties still matter. The SPDI Rules require a published privacy policy. They treat passwords and financial, health, sexual-orientation, medical and biometric information as sensitive personal data/information. Some collection and disclosure rules may apply differently to a processor acting under a contract. That does not give the processor permission to reuse data for its own purposes. See the [SPDI Rules](https://upload.indiacode.nic.in/showfile?actid=AC_CEN_45_76_00001_200021_1517807324077&filename=GSR313E_10511%281%29_0.pdf&type=rule).

Build strong DPDP-style controls now. Use a published notice and policy, follow client instructions and limit each use to its stated purpose. Collect only the minimum fields. Provide opt-out, access and security controls. Use vendor contracts, a breach process and deletion. Keep Aadhaar/PAN/bank statements and exact income, health and biometric data out of the lead-response layer.

The transition has an end date. Under the notification reviewed, DPDP Act section 44(2) starts on 13 May 2027 and omits IT Act section 43A. Counsel should identify which current SPDI controls will continue after that date through contracts or other laws.

### 30.14 DPDP target operating model for 13 May 2027

The developer or client will usually be the Data Fiduciary. You will be a Data Processor only if your real conduct and contract follow the client’s written instructions. The Data Fiduciary stays responsible and may use a processor only under a valid contract. If you reuse leads on your own for cross-selling, benchmarking, your marketing or model training, you may take on separate Fiduciary duties. You may also break the client’s trust.

| Duty/control | Build now |
|---|---|
| Notice | Give a separate, plain notice. List the personal data and its purpose or service. Give a business contact, a route for withdrawal and rights, and a way to complain to the Board |
| Consent | Consent must be free, specific, informed, unconditional and clear. Require an affirmative action and keep proof |
| Withdrawal | Make withdrawal as easy as consent. Stop future related processing and outreach, then apply the change everywhere |
| Legitimate uses | The client and legal owner must decide. Never use this as a loophole for bought lists |
| Accuracy | Provide a correction workflow. Use approved project facts and sources |
| Security | Use encryption or masking, access controls, logs and monitoring, backups and continuity, contracts and organisational controls |
| Breach | Support notices to affected people and the Board. Meet initial and detailed timelines, and escalate to the client |
| Rights | Under client instructions, support search, an access summary, correction, erasure and grievances |
| Children | Treat anyone under 18 as a child. Do not target or qualify them. Send uncertain cases to the client or a human. Any allowed processing needs verified parental consent. It must also follow limits on harmful processing, tracking or behavioural monitoring and targeted advertising |
| Vendors/transfers | Name each subprocessor. Record its purpose, location and terms. Give notice of changes and define exit steps |
| Erasure/retention | Build for erasure when the purpose ends or consent is withdrawn, plus any retention required by statute or law |

Final Rule 6 lists minimum safeguards. They include encryption, obfuscation, masking or tokenisation; access controls; logs and monitoring; continuity and backups; processor contract security; and organisational measures. Rule 7 requires notice to each affected person without delay. It also requires an initial notice to the Board without delay and detailed information for the Board within 72 hours. These duties start with the relevant operating rules on 13 May 2027.

### 30.15 DPDP retention tension

Final Rules 6(1)(e) and 8(3) include one-year retention wording for relevant logs, personal data and processing records. The Act also includes rules that limit use to a purpose and require erasure. Do not guess how these rules fit together. Design this flow:

```text
keep an active operating record only while the purpose or law requires it
→ suppress outreach at once when consent is withdrawn or the purpose ends
→ keep a separate, restricted statutory/security record only where required
→ purge it automatically after the applicable minimum period or other legal hold
```

Before May 2027, ask privacy counsel to approve the data categories, how Rule 8 applies, security access and the deletion schedule.

### 30.16 CERT-In duties

The CERT-In directions dated 28 April 2022 cover service providers, intermediaries, body corporates and others. They require covered entities to sync ICT clocks to time traceable to NIC/NPL. They must name and update a CERT-In point of contact. They must securely keep all ICT logs in India for a rolling 180 days. They must report Annexure-I cyber incidents within six hours after they notice one or learn of one. Examples include unauthorised access, phishing, malware or ransomware, a data breach or leak, and attacks on cloud or AI systems. [CERT-In Directions](https://www.cert-in.org.in/PDF/CERT-In_Directions_70B_28.04.2022.pdf).

Operational controls:

- Before launch, name the PoC and a 24×7 reachable escalation contact;
- Keep a 180-day ICT log system in India. Align it with data minimisation and access rules;
- Record the exact time of awareness. Use an incident-classification checklist;
- Set a client and vendor alert target of no more than one hour so the six-hour deadline is still possible;
- For a qualifying incident, report the facts known within six hours. Do not wait until the root cause is certain;
- Add more information as the investigation continues;
- Preserve evidence and an NTP-synchronised timeline;
- Run a tabletop test every quarter and after a major change.

The Directions generally took effect on 27 June 2022. CERT-In extended the effective date to 25 September 2022 for qualifying MSMEs. That same date applied separately to certain customer-validation duties for data-centre, VPS, cloud and VPN providers. These duties are current. Check the [CERT-In directions/FAQ page](https://www.cert-in.org.in/Directions70B.jsp) for updates.

### 30.17 Incident reporting decision tree

```text
a security or privacy event is suspected
→ contain only what is needed + keep evidence + record the awareness time
→ notify the client, privacy, security and legal owners, and the affected vendor
→ is it an Annexure-I CERT-In incident? if yes, report it within 6 hours
→ on or after 13 May 2027, is it a DPDP breach? support immediate notice to affected people and the Board
   and give the Board detailed information within 72 hours
→ meet all other contract, regulator, insurer and law-enforcement duties
→ recover, reconcile, document the event and prevent it from happening again
```

Your DPA must not let a contract approval process delay any statutory duty that belongs to you.

### 30.18 PMLA/AML escalation

G.S.R. 798(E), dated 28 December 2020, covers a person who provides services related to the sale or purchase of real estate. It applies when that person has annual turnover of ₹20 lakh or more. It treats that person as carrying on a designated business or profession and therefore as a reporting entity. This PMLA test is separate from other tests. MahaRERA registration is a strong reason to review the issue, but it is neither required nor enough on its own. The ₹20 lakh test uses turnover, not profit. If the rule covers you, the duties are mandatory. They include FINnet 2.0 registration and, where applicable, a Principal Officer and Designated Director. They also include AML risk and policy controls, plus checks on clients and beneficial owners. Other duties include required and suspicious-transaction reports, non-tipping-off and usually five-year record retention. Hire an AML lawyer or CA to review the business at ₹15 lakh annualised turnover. This gives time to prepare before the threshold. Start with [FIU-IND AML legislation/notifications](https://fiuindia.gov.in/files/AML_Legislation/notification.html) and current PMLA material.

Do not perform KYC or collect identity or financial documents unless counsel confirms that you must do so and a secure, approved process is ready.

### 30.19 GST/RERA interaction

Being an agent, earning commission or acting as an intermediary can change several tax and legal results. These include the GST place of supply, classification and rate, export treatment, income-tax presumptive eligibility and PMLA analysis. The label “monthly retainer” does not decide the issue. RERA agent, PMLA real-estate service, GST intermediary, income-tax agency or commission and contractual agent are separate statutory tests. One classification is evidence, but it does not automatically decide another. Chapter 31 covers finance controls. The CA needs the real SOW and details of buyer-facing conduct.

### 30.20 MSA/SOW/DPA minimum compliance clauses

| Clause | Minimum content |
|---|---|
| Scope/red lines | State the exact response, qualification and scheduling work. Ban negotiation, deposits, allotment, binding promises, legal/finance/investment advice and unapproved facts |
| Client warranty | Require lawful leads, project and advertising authority, agent association, permission, client ownership of WABA/DLT/CRM and approved content |
| Data roles/instructions | State the fields, people, purpose and period. Ban sale, independent profiling, reuse across clients and model training |
| Subprocessors | Name vendors, locations and purposes. Require advance notice of changes, equal duties and a way to object or exit |
| Security | List exact controls for access, isolation, logs, patching, backups, incidents and evidence |
| Incident | Require notice from vendor → client at once and no later than one hour. Require preservation and help. No clause may block statutory reporting |
| Rights/retention | Support search, export, correction, erasure and suppression. Cover legal holds, backups and a return or deletion certificate |
| Campaign manifest | State the exact phase; registration, agent and QR details; claims and templates; consent; dates; and approvers |
| IP/AI | Cover client data and content, background IP, no training, model review, provenance and a human path |
| Legal/commercial | State independent-contractor status and no authority. Allow an illegal campaign to be paused. Have a professional review liability and indemnity carve-outs |

### 30.21 Absolute compliance rejection list

Reject or stop work when a client asks for any of these:

- Scraped, purchased or recycled leads without permission you can defend;
- Cold WhatsApp messages, bulk senders or unofficial browser automation;
- Promotional calls from personal SIMs or ordinary numbers to avoid DLT/DND;
- Contact after opt-out, or calling promotional content a “service”;
- Work on an unregistered, expired or wrong project phase;
- Missing RERA, agent or QR disclosures where required;
- A fake price, inventory claim, approval, urgency, return, possession date or testimonial;
- Hidden AI, voice cloning or secret recording;
- Property recommendations, negotiation, deposits or sale commission without a cleared RERA, PMLA and tax model;
- Reuse, resale or model training on buyer data for your own purposes;
- Production access without a written scope, client ownership and minimum security;
- Delaying a legally required incident report to protect reputation.

### 30.22 Pre-live compliance gate

| Gate | Evidence |
|---|---|
| Employment | Review the contract and policies. Get an NOC if required. Separate the device, accounts and work time |
| RERA classification | Get a written Maharashtra opinion. Complete registration and certification if work faces buyers |
| Project | Confirm the live, exact phase, promoter authority, association, registration, QR and content versions |
| Lead provenance | Keep the source, named business and project, exact notice, consent action and time, and revocation history |
| WhatsApp | Use the client’s WABA, valid opt-in, approved templates, 24-hour logic, a human path and STOP handling |
| SMS/voice | Confirm the PE, header, templates, registered chain, scrub and 140/TSP decision |
| Privacy | Publish the policy and notice. Set the DPA, minimum fields, vendors, rights and retention roadmap |
| Security | Set MFA, least privilege, secrets, isolation, logs, backups, a kill switch and offboarding |
| Incident | Set the CERT-In PoC, India 180-day log plan, six-hour playbook and tabletop test |
| Tax/AML | Get CA decisions on classification, GST and books. Set the PMLA review trigger |
| Pilot | Use one client, one registered phase and a small cohort with consent. Require full human review |

### 30.23 Quarterly update checklist

Every quarter, recheck MahaRERA orders and project and agent registrations. Recheck TRAI consolidated regulations and directions, plus provider confirmation. Recheck WhatsApp policy, pricing and templates. Recheck DPDP start dates and Board guidance. Also recheck CERT-In directions and advisories, IT Rules and AI guidance, and GST, Income Tax and PMLA changes. Recheck vendor subprocessors and data locations, plus client permissions and content. Record the URL, access date, owner, change, decision and affected workflows and tests.
